\Lambda_{n}
concentrates around
(n/\ell)\cdot\mathbb{P}^{\scriptscriptstyle 0}(|{\mathcal{C}}(0)|=\ell)
. Hence,

(3.9)

1-\frac{|{\mathcal{C}}_{n}^{{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}}|}{n}\approx\sum_{\ell\geq 1}\mathbb{P}^{\scriptscriptstyle 0}\big(|{\mathcal{C}}(0)|=\ell\big)\cdot(1-p)^{h\ell}=\mathbb{E}^{\scriptscriptstyle 0}\big[(1-p)^{h|{\mathcal{C}}(0)|}\big].

On the event
\{|{\mathcal{C}}_{n}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}|/n>\rho\}
,
the right-hand side above should be at most
1-\rho
. Thus, letting
h
to be the smallest integer satisfying

(3.10)

\mathbb{E}^{\scriptscriptstyle 0}\big[(1-p)^{h|{\mathcal{C}}(0)|}\big]\leq 1-\rho,

the presence of
h
hubs implies the event
\{|{\mathcal{C}}_{n}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}|/n>\rho\}
.
The solution to (
3.10
) with equality is exactly
\mathrm{hubs}(\rho)
in (
2.17
).

Concentration on the number of size-
\ell
components.

To move from (
3.8
) to (
3.9
), we need that the rate of concentration of the number of size-
\ell
components is
o(n^{-(\tau-2)h}))
. Local convergence (LC) does not provide any convergence rate. We boost the performance of LC by applying it to disjoint subboxes of
\Lambda_{n}
, binomial concentration, and a bound on the number of components containing edges that cross box-boundaries.
When vertex-marks are at most a linear function in
n
, this yields a polynomial convergence rate with a tunable exponent.

Linear-scale hubs are required to increase the giant’s size.

For the lower bound in (
2.19
), we use that the graph without hubs contains a giant component of density almost
\theta
by the lower tail of large deviations. The hubs connect to this giant with error probability exponential in
n
. When
\mathrm{hubs}(\rho)\notin{\mathbb{N}}
, our concentration bound on size-
\ell
-component is strong enough to guarantee that the presence of
\lceil\mathrm{hubs}(\rho)\rceil
hubs increases the giant’s size to a value above
\rho n
. We also show that any event that has fewer than
\lceil\mathrm{hubs}(\rho)\rceil
hubs means that too many size-
\ell
components remain outside of the giant with probability at least
\Theta\big(n^{-(\tau-2)h}\big)
. So,
the complement of the giant remains larger than
(1-\rho)n
on such events. This gives the upper bound.

Homogeneous percolation models.
In Bernoulli bond percolation or long-range percolation on
{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}
, the degree-distribution has (super-)exponential tails, so no linear-scale hubs are present. We show that the number of size-
\ell
components concentrate with error probability exponentially small in
n
for each constant
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
. We capture the giant’s size by its complement, and
Theorem

2.4
follows.

4.
Existence of a giant

The main goal of this section is to prove the existence of a giant, i.e., a linear-sized component, for the whole supercritical regime when
\max(\zeta_{\mathrm{hl}},\zeta_{\mathrm{ll}})>0
. For
\zeta_{\mathrm{hh}}>0
, we already proved existence in our recent work
[
48
]
.
The first proposition corresponds to (
3.2
) in the roadmap in Section
3
.

Proposition 4.1

(Existence of a giant)
.

Consider a supercritical KSRG satisfying Assumption
2.2
with a Poisson point process as vertex set. Assume that the parameters satisfy
\max(\zeta_{\mathrm{hl}},\zeta_{\mathrm{ll}})>0
.
For each constant
\delta>0
, there exists a constant
A>0
such that for all
n\geq 1
,

(hl)

when
\zeta_{\mathrm{hl}}>0
,

(4.1)

\mathbb{P}\big(|{\mathcal{C}}_{n}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}[1,An^{\gamma_{\mathrm{hl}}})|\leq\tfrac{1}{A}n\big)\leq\exp\big(-\tfrac{1}{A}n^{\zeta_{\mathrm{hl}}-\delta}\big),

(ll)

when
\zeta_{\mathrm{ll}}>0
,

(4.2)

\mathbb{P}\big(|{\mathcal{C}}_{n}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}[1,A)|\leq\tfrac{1}{A}n\big)\leq\exp\big(-\tfrac{1}{A}n^{\zeta_{\mathrm{ll}}-\delta}\big).

The statements (
4.1
) and (
4.2
) remain valid for the Palm version
\mathbb{P}^{\scriptscriptstyle x}
of
\mathbb{P}
for any
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
.

Proposition

4.1
generalizes
[
14
, Theorem 3.2]
by Biskup from long-range percolation on
{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}
to a wider class of KSRGs. The presence of vertex marks in KSRGs, and the truncation of them in the statement of Proposition
4.1
require new ideas.
We will prove Proposition
4.1
via a multi-scale renormalization scheme. To initialize this scheme, we need an initial lower bound on
|{\mathcal{C}}_{n}^{{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}}|
.

Lemma 4.2

(Existence of an “almost”-giant)
.

Consider a supercritical KSRG under the same setting as in Proposition
4.1
. For all
\varepsilon>0

(4.3)

\mathbb{P}\big(|{\mathcal{C}}_{n}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}|\leq n^{1-\varepsilon}\big)\longrightarrow 0,\qquad\mbox{ as }n\to\infty.

The statement remains valid for the Palm version
\mathbb{P}^{\scriptscriptstyle x}
of
\mathbb{P}
for any
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
.

The proof of this lemma is inspired by a multi-scale renormalization technique introduced for long-range percolation by Berger in
[
12
]
. When
\zeta_{\mathrm{ll}}=2-\alpha>0
and the vertex set is formed by a PPP,
[
37
, Proposition 3.9]
already proves (
4.3
) using an adaptation of
[
12
]
.
For the case
\zeta_{\mathrm{hl}}>0
we give a new proof in Appendix
B
that explicitly relies on the vertex-marks. The rest of this section is devoted to proving Proposition
4.1
. We first introduce some additional preliminaries.

4.1.
Preliminaries

We describe a sprinkling technique that enables us to reveal the vertex set of a KSRG on a PPP in multiple stages, so that the graph is supercritical already before sprinkling.

Definition 4.3

(Scaled-thinned KSRG on a PPP)
.

Consider a KSRG on a PPP.
Let
q\in[0,1]
and
\eta>0
. We write
\eta{\mathcal{G}}_{n}^{\beta,q}
for a scaled-thinned KSRG as follows: first, we keep each vertex of the PPP
{\mathcal{V}}
independently with probability
q
. Then we sample the edges according to
Definition

2.1
with edge-density parameter
\beta>0
. Finally, we we assign a new location to each retained vertex, i.e., we map
u=(x_{u},w_{u})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times[1,\infty)
to
(\eta x_{u},w_{u})
.

It is not hard to see that the scaled-thinned KSRG with
\eta=q
is distributed like the original graph with a smaller parameter
\beta
in a smaller box. This is the content of the following observation.

Observation 4.4

(Sprinkling trick-A)
.

Consider a supercritical KSRG on a PPP, i.e., with
\beta>\beta_{c}(p,\alpha,\tau,\sigma)
. Let
q\in(0,1]
. Then the following distributional identity holds:

(4.4)

q{\mathcal{G}}_{n}^{\beta,q}\overset{d}{=}{\mathcal{G}}_{qn}^{\beta q^{d},1}\overset{d}{=}{\mathcal{G}}_{qn}^{\beta q^{d}}

In particular, the graph
q{\mathcal{G}}_{n}^{\beta,q}
is supercritical whenever the new edge-intensity
\beta q^{d}>\beta_{c}
.

Proof.

Denote by
{\mathcal{V}}^{(q,q)}
the marked vertex set of
q{\mathcal{G}}_{n}^{\beta,q}
. Since the projection of
{\mathcal{V}}
onto the spatial dimensions in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
is a PPP with intensity being the Lebesgue measure, thinning
{\mathcal{V}}
gives intensity
q\mathrm{Leb}(\cdot)
. Then, re-scaling with
\eta=q
maps the box
\Lambda_{n}
to
\Lambda_{qn}
. For any set
A\subseteq\Lambda_{qn}
, the expected number of points in it is
q\mathrm{Leb}(\eta^{-1}A)=\mathrm{Leb}(A)
. Hence
{\mathcal{V}}^{(q,q)}
has the same distribution as
{\mathcal{V}}\cap\Lambda_{qn}
, since the spatial intensity of the new point process
{\mathcal{V}}^{(q,q)}
is
\mathrm{Leb}(\cdot)
again, and the distribution of the mark-coordinate is unchanged.
We now inspect the connection probabilities in
q{\mathcal{G}}_{n}^{\beta,q}
. Let us denote the new connectivity function by
\mathrm{p}^{(q,q)}
. Consider two points
u=(x_{u},w_{u})
and
v=(x_{v},w_{v})
in
{\mathcal{V}}^{(q,q)}
. Since we connect
u
to
v
by an edge in
q{\mathcal{G}}_{n}^{\beta,q}
iff we connect their inverse points
(q^{-1}x_{u},w_{u})
and
(q^{-1}x_{v},w_{v})
in
{\mathcal{G}}_{n}
by an edge, the new connection probability
\mathrm{p}^{(q,q)}
is given by

\mathrm{p}^{(q,q)}\big((x_{u},w_{u}),(x_{v},w_{v})\big)=\mathrm{p}\big((q^{-1}x_{u},w_{u}),(q^{-1}x_{v},w_{v})\big)=\varphi\Big(\beta\frac{\kappa(w_{u},w_{v})}{\|q^{-1}(x_{u}-x_{v})\|^{d}}\Big).

Thus, the new graph has the same distribution as a graph on
{\mathcal{V}}\cap\Lambda_{qn}
with the same connection probabilities but new edge-density parameter
\beta^{\prime}=\beta q^{d}
. This graph is denoted by
{\mathcal{G}}_{qn}^{\beta^{\prime},1}
, since in this latter notation the thinning factor is
q=1
, and the box is
\Lambda_{qn}
. The last identity in (
4.4
) says that omitting
q=1
from the superscript is consistent with the notation.
Finally, the new graph is supercritical whenever the new edge-intensity
\beta q^{d}
is at least
\beta_{c}
as well.
∎

Throughout the paper we will use the following construction of KSRGs when we consider PPPs as the underlying vertex set, following the setting of Assumption
2.2
.

Definition 4.5

(Alternative KSRG construction on a PPP)
.

Consider a KSRG on a PPP with parameters and kernel
\kappa
from Assumption
2.2
. If
\beta>\beta_{c}
, let
q\in(0,1]
be such that
\beta q^{d}>\beta_{c}
, i.e., the scaled-thinned graph
q{\mathcal{G}}_{n}^{\beta,q}
in (
4.4
) is supercritical. If
\beta\leq\beta_{c}
, set
q=1
.
Let
{\mathcal{V}}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{base}}
and
{\mathcal{V}}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{spr}}
be two independent PPPs with intensity
q\mathrm{Leb}\!\times\!F_{W}(\mathrm{d}w)
and
(1-q)\mathrm{Leb}\!\times\!F_{W}(\mathrm{d}w)
, respectively, with
F_{W}
as in Assumption
2.2
. Define

\overline{{\mathcal{V}}}:={\mathcal{V}}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{base}}\cup{\mathcal{V}}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{spr}},

which is equal in distribution to a Poisson point process on
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times[1,\infty)
with intensity

(4.5)

\mathrm{Leb}\times F_{W}(\mathrm{d}w)=\begin{cases}\mathrm{d}x\times(\tau-1)w^{-\tau}\mathrm{d}w,&\text{if }\tau<\infty,\\
\mathrm{d}x\times\delta_{1}(\mathrm{d}w),&\text{if }\tau=\infty,\end{cases}

where
\delta_{1}(\cdot)
denotes a Dirac measure at one.
Conditionally on the realization of
\overline{{\mathcal{V}}}
, let
\overline{{\mathcal{G}}}_{n}^{\scriptscriptstyle\beta}
be the graph on
\overline{{\mathcal{V}}}
where two vertices
u,v\in\overline{{\mathcal{V}}}
are connected by an edge independently with probability

(4.6)

\mathrm{p}(u,v)=\begin{cases}p\Big(\beta\cdot\frac{\kappa(w_{u},w_{v})}{\|x_{u}-x_{v}\|^{d}}\wedge 1\Big)^{\alpha},&\text{if }\alpha<\infty,\\
p\mathds{1}\big\{\beta\kappa(w_{u},w_{v})\geq\|x_{u}-x_{v}\|^{d}\big\},&\text{if }\alpha=\infty.\end{cases}

Let
{\mathcal{G}}_{n}^{\beta,{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{base}}}\ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{d}}}\ {\mathcal{G}}_{n}^{\beta,q}
be the induced subgraph on
{\mathcal{V}}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{base}}
. If
q<1
, we call
{\mathcal{G}}_{n}^{\scriptscriptstyle\beta,\mathrm{base}}
the
thinned
KSRG.
We omit the superscript
\beta
if it is clear from the context.

Observation 4.6

(Sprinkling trick-B)
.

Consider a supercritical KSRG on a PPP, and let
\overline{{\mathcal{G}}}_{n}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\beta}}
and
{\mathcal{G}}_{n}^{\beta,{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{base}}}
be as in
Definition

4.5
. Write
\beta^{\prime}:=\beta q^{d}
.
Let
{\mathcal{C}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}_{n,{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{base}}}
and
{\mathcal{C}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}_{qn,\beta^{\prime}}
be the largest component in
{\mathcal{G}}_{n}^{\beta,{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{base}}}
and
{\mathcal{G}}_{qn}^{\beta^{\prime}}
, respectively. Then

\overline{{\mathcal{G}}}_{n}^{\scriptscriptstyle\beta}\overset{d}{=}{\mathcal{G}}_{n}^{\scriptscriptstyle\beta},\qquad{\mathcal{G}}_{n}^{\beta,{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{base}}}\overset{d}{=}\tfrac{1}{q}{\mathcal{G}}_{qn}^{\beta^{\prime}},\qquad|{\mathcal{C}}_{qn,\beta^{\prime}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}|\,\overset{d}{=}\,|{\mathcal{C}}_{n,{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{base}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}|.

Proof.

\overline{{\mathcal{G}}}_{n}^{\scriptscriptstyle\beta}
has the same law as
{\mathcal{G}}_{n}^{\scriptscriptstyle\beta}
since
\overline{{\mathcal{V}}}
has the same law as
{\mathcal{V}}
and the connection probabilities are the same.
{\mathcal{G}}_{n}^{\beta,{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{base}}}
has the same law as
1{\mathcal{G}}_{n}^{\beta,q}
in
Definition

4.3
, i.e., we just apply thinning but do not rescale. We write this now as
q^{-1}(q{\mathcal{G}}_{n}^{\beta,q})
and apply the identity (
4.4
) to the scaled-thinned graph, and we get the second distributional identity above. The last identity about the largest component holds since edges (and thus connected components) do not change under rescaling.
∎

Let us also state a well-known statement about the connectivity of Erdős-Rényi random graphs. We give a short (suboptimal) proof for the sake of completeness.

Claim 4.7

(Connectivity of
G(n,p_{n})
)
.

Consider the Erdős-Rényi random graph
G(n,p_{n})
with
np_{n}=\omega(\log n)
. For all sufficiently large
n
,

(4.7)

\mathbb{P}\big(\mbox{$G(n,p_{n})$ is not connected}\big)\leq 3({\mathrm{e}}n)^{2}(1-p_{n})^{n/2}.

Proof.

If
G(n,p_{n})
is not connected, then there must exist a connected component of size
k
, for some
1\leq k\leq\lfloor n/2\rfloor
. For
k=1
, this probability is at most
n(1-p_{n})^{n-1}\leq({\mathrm{e}}n)^{2}(1-p_{n})^{n/2}
by a union bound over all vertices. For
k\geq 2
, by Cayley’s formula, there are
k^{k-2}
spanning trees on
k
vertices, and a connected component of size
k
has to contain such a tree. So by a union bound over the spanning trees, the probability that a connected component of size between
2
and
n/2
exists without being connected to the other
n-k
vertices, is at most

\sum_{k=2}^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\binom{n}{k}k^{k-2}(1-p_{n})^{k(n-k)}\leq\sum_{k=2}^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\left(\frac{{\mathrm{e}}n}{k}\right)^{k}k^{k-2}(1-p_{n})^{k(n-k)}\leq\sum_{k=2}^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\big({\mathrm{e}}n(1-p_{n})^{n-k}\big)^{k}.

Using
n-k\geq\lceil n/2\rceil
, we get that
the right-hand side is bounded from above by

\sum_{k=2}^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\big({\mathrm{e}}n(1-p_{n})^{\lceil n/2\rceil}\big)^{k}\leq\frac{({\mathrm{e}}n)^{2}(1-p_{n})^{2\lceil n/2\rceil}}{1-{\mathrm{e}}n(1-p_{n})^{\lceil n/2\rceil}}\leq 2({\mathrm{e}}n)^{2}(1-p_{n})^{n/2}.

Combining the cases of
k=1
and
k\geq 2
we obtain the desired statement.
∎

4.2.
A linear-sized component (high-low regime)

We turn to the proof of Proposition
4.1
for the hl-regime, so we restrict to KSRGs on PPPs. We describe the adjustments for the ll-regime at the end of this section. A sketch of the argument can be found in Figure
3
.

Figure 3
.
Sketch of the multi-scale renormalization for the hl-regime. The level-
(i-1)
box situated second from the left is bad because its largest connected component is too small (e.g. due to failure of
Con
at a previous level). The fifth box is bad because the
Mark
step failed at level
i-1
, even though it contains a large component. The large components in the first, third, and fourth box are connected using the connector vertices, forming the darkred component. At the end, we reveal the high-mark vertices in
[n_{i}^{\gamma},2n_{i}^{\gamma})
and their edges to the local giant in the level-i box. If there are sufficiently many high-mark vertices, the box passes the
Mark
-step. We will only use these vertices in the next level.

4.2.1.
Multi-scale renormalization – sketch

We set up an iterative boxing scheme. The initial box size is a constant
n_{0}
. We check if the largest component of a thinned KSRGs inside a level-
0
box has size at least
\rho_{0}n_{0}:=n_{0}^{1-\varepsilon}
. Next, we sprinkle vertices of sufficiently high (but constant) mark that connect to this largest component. If both of these steps succeed, we call a level-
0
box good. This happens with constant probability using
Lemma

4.2
.

For each iteration
i\geq 1
, we group
m_{i}
level-
(i-1)
boxes of volume
n_{i-1}
into a larger level-
i
box
\mathrm{B}
of volume
n_{i}:=m_{i}n_{i-1}
. We always work with only the ‘good’ subboxes of the previous level
i-1
inside
\mathrm{B}
to determine whether the level-
i
box
\mathrm{B}
is good,
and control the probabilities of the following three events:

(Sub)

Using independence across subboxes, at least
(1-\varepsilon_{i})m_{i}
of the level-
(i-1)
subboxes of volume
n_{i-1}
of
\mathrm{B}
are level-
(i-1)
-good with sufficiently large probability.
That is, the subboxes satisfy the next events
(Con), (Mark)
at level
i-1
: they have a local component of density at least
\rho_{i-1}
, which contains enough level-
(i-1)
high-mark vertices.

(Con)

Each good level-
(i\!-\!1)
subbox of
\mathrm{B}
has enough high-mark vertices inside a local component by the event
(Mark)
on the previous level. We use these high-mark vertices to connect the level-
(i\!-\!1)
subboxes
to each other
. This is done by a stochastic domination of an Erdős-Rényi random graph
G\big((1-\varepsilon_{i})m_{i},q_{i}\big)
: the giant component of each good level-
(i-1)
subbox is represented by a vertex in
G
. We ensure that
q_{i}
is above the connectivity threshold of Erdős-Rényi random graphs. The outcome is that the level-
i
box
\mathrm{B}
contains a connected component of density at least
\rho_{i}:=\rho_{i-1}(1-\varepsilon_{i})
with error probability stretched exponential in
n_{i}
(and not
n_{i-1}
that we had on level
i-1
).

(Mark)

The sprinkling trick from Section
4.1
allows us to sprinkle level-
i
high-mark vertices that were not exposed in previous levels. We make sure enough of them connect to the large connected component in
\mathrm{B}
built in the
Con
-step. We will then use these vertices in the
Con
-step of level
i\!+\!1
later.

If the steps
Con
and
Mark
succeed then we call
\mathrm{B}
level-
i
good. (The first step
Sub
is only a tool to control error probabilities of these latter steps).
We will ensure that
\inf_{i}\rho_{i}>0
, so the size of the connected component we construct in the
Con
-step is linear in
n_{i}
. Currently we use
Lemma

4.2
for initializing the level-
0
boxes: this guarantees local largest components of size
n_{0}^{1-\varepsilon}
, i.e., density
n_{0}^{-\varepsilon}
, and error probability tending to
0
at an unspecified rate. This proof of Proposition
4.1
here will ‘boost’ the giant to have density at least of order
n_{0}^{-\varepsilon}n_{i}
, which is linear in
n_{i}
(as
i\to\infty
, because
n_{0}
is a constant), and the error probability becomes stretched exponential in
n_{i}
. This renormalization gives Proposition
4.1
along the subsequence
(n_{i})_{i\geq 0}
of box sizes.
A final boxing argument will ensure that Proposition
4.1
holds for all
n\geq 1
.

4.2.2.
Formal setup of the renormalization

We formalize the boxing scheme with all its parameters.
Fix any small constant
\delta>0
that appears in the statement of Proposition
4.1
. We define an extra constant, that shall describe the growth rate of the box sizes
(n_{i})_{i\geq 1}
:

(4.8)

\xi_{\delta}:=\xi_{\delta}(\zeta)=\frac{\delta/2}{\zeta+(\alpha-1)-\delta/2}.

The value of
\xi_{\delta}
is defined both for
\zeta_{\mathrm{hl}}=(\tau-1)/\alpha-(\tau-2)
and for
\zeta_{\mathrm{ll}}=2-\alpha
whenever these are positive.
Since
\alpha>1
,
\xi_{\delta}>0
for all
\delta<\zeta
.
Unless explicitly stated otherwise, we abbreviate in the rest of the section

(4.9)

\gamma=\gamma_{\mathrm{hl}}=1-1/\alpha,\qquad\zeta=\zeta_{\mathrm{hl}}=(\tau-1)/\alpha-(\tau-2).

We recall the PPPs
{\mathcal{V}}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{base}}
and
{\mathcal{V}}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{spr}}
with thinning constant
q<1
from
Definition

4.5
. We will choose any
q
with
\beta q^{d}>\beta_{c}
. We recall the parameters
p,\beta
from the connectivity function in (
4.6
). We define one extra constant depending on
q
:

(4.10)

\delta^{\prime}:=\tfrac{1}{4}(1-q)(1-2^{-(\tau-1)})\cdot p\beta^{\alpha}d^{-\alpha d/2}.

Slightly abusing notation, if
{\mathcal{V}}
is a set of vertices and
{\mathcal{C}}
is a component, we write
{\mathcal{V}}\cap{\mathcal{C}}
for the set of vertices in
{\mathcal{V}}
that belong to the component
{\mathcal{C}}
.

Definition 4.8

(renormalization scheme)
.

Consider a KSRG under the same setting as in Proposition
4.1
.
Fix
\zeta>0
, and
\delta\in(0,\zeta/2)
, and
\xi_{\delta}(\zeta)
as in (
4.8
), and a thinning constant
q<1
with
\beta q^{d}>\beta_{c}
.
Let
n_{0}
be a large constant. We iteratively define for
i\geq 1

(4.11)

m_{i}:=\left\lfloor n_{i-1}^{\xi_{\delta}/d}\right\rfloor^{d},\qquad n_{i}:=m_{i}n_{i-1}.

Let
{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}(x)
denote a box centered at
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
of volume
n_{i}
. We define the level-
i
vertex set of
{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}(x)
to be

(4.12)

\widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{i}:={\mathcal{V}}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{base}}_{{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}(x)}\big[1,n_{0}^{2/(\tau-1)}\big)\cup{\mathcal{V}}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{spr}}_{{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}(x)}[1,2n_{i}^{\gamma}).

We write
\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{n_{i}}
for the induced subgraph on
\widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
.
Define the sequences

(4.13)

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{i}

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}1-n_{0}^{-\delta/4},&\text{if }i=0,\\
(i+1)^{-2},&\text{if }i\geq 1,\end{cases}

(4.14)

\displaystyle\rho_{i}

\displaystyle:=\prod_{j=0}^{i}(1-\varepsilon_{j})=n_{0}^{-\delta/4}\prod_{j=2}^{i+1}(1-j^{-2}),\qquad\rho:=\lim_{j\to\infty}\rho_{j}.

We call
{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}(x)

i
-good (and
i
-bad otherwise) if it satisfies the event (with
\delta^{\prime}
in (
4.10
))

(4.15)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{i\textnormal{-}good}}(x):=\Big\{\exists

\displaystyle\text{comp.\ ${\mathcal{C}}_{i}$ in }\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{n_{i}}:|{\mathcal{C}}_{i}|\geq\rho_{i}n_{i},

\displaystyle|{\mathcal{V}}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{spr}}_{{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}(x)}[n_{i}^{\gamma},2n_{i}^{\gamma})\cap{\mathcal{C}}_{i}|\geq\delta^{\prime}\rho_{i}n_{i}^{\zeta}\Big\}.

The definitions of
(m_{i})_{i\geq 0}
and
(n_{i})_{i\geq 0}
in (
4.11
) ensure that they increase doubly exponentially in
i
: indeed, ignoring integer parts,
n_{i}\approx n_{i-1}^{1+\xi_{\delta}}
so
n_{i}\approx n_{0}^{(1+\xi_{\delta})^{i}}
. This also implies that
2n_{i-1}^{\gamma}<n_{i}^{\gamma}
, and so for any level-
(i\!-\!1)
subbox centered at any
y
,
\widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{i-1,y}
is disjoint of
{\mathcal{V}}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{spr}}_{{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}(x)}[n_{i}^{\gamma},2n_{i}^{\gamma})
. Thus, in
{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}(x)
we sprinkle in
new
vertices with mark in
[n_{i}^{\gamma},2n_{i}^{\gamma})
in level-
i
compared to previous levels. Summability of
(\varepsilon_{i})_{i\geq 0}
in (
4.13
) implies that
\rho>0
for any starting value
\varepsilon_{0}(n_{0})
in (
4.13
–
4.14
).

The next claim provides useful bounds on the sequences that will appear in subsequent computations. Actually,
\xi_{\delta}
in (
4.8
) is chosen precisely such that (
4.16
)–(
4.18
) are satisfied below. The label on the inequalities indicate where we will use the bounds later.

Claim 4.9

(Useful bounds)
.

Fix
any

\zeta\in(0,1)
and then
\xi_{\delta}(\zeta)
in (
4.8
).
Assume
\delta\in(0,\zeta/2)
and consider the quantities in
Definition

4.8
. For each constant
C>0
, there exists a constant
n_{0}^{\star}>0
such that for all
n_{0}\geq n_{0}^{\star}
and all
i\geq 1
, the following hold:

(4.16)

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{i}m_{i}n_{i-1}^{\zeta-\delta}

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{(Sub)}}{\geq}Cn_{i}^{\zeta-\delta},

(4.17)

\displaystyle\rho_{i-1}^{2}m_{i}^{1-\alpha}n_{i-1}^{\zeta}

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{(Con)}}{\geq}Cn_{i}^{\zeta-\delta},

(4.18)

\displaystyle\rho_{i}n_{i}^{\zeta}

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{(Mark)}}{\geq}Cn_{i}^{\zeta-\delta}.

Further,

(4.19)

\displaystyle n_{i}\in\Big[n_{0}^{(1+\xi_{\delta}/2)^{i}},n_{0}^{(1+\xi_{\delta})^{i}}\Big],\qquad m_{i}\in\Big[\tfrac{1}{2}n_{0}^{\xi_{\delta}(1+\xi_{\delta}/2)^{i-1}},n_{0}^{\xi_{\delta}(1+\xi_{\delta})^{i-1}}\Big],

(4.20)

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{i}m_{i}^{1-\zeta+\delta}\geq C,\qquad(n_{i-1}/n_{0})^{\zeta-\delta}\geq C\log({\mathrm{e}}/\varepsilon_{i}).

Moreover, for all
m\leq m_{i+1}
and
n:=n_{i}m
,

(4.21)

mn_{i}^{\zeta+\alpha}n^{-\alpha}\geq n^{\zeta-\delta}.

Finally, when
\zeta=\zeta_{\mathrm{hl}}
and
\gamma=1-1/\alpha
, then

(4.22)

n_{i}^{\gamma}>2n_{i-1}^{\gamma}.

We postpone the proof to Appendix
B.2
.

4.2.3.
Induction base of the renormalization

We now carry out the multi-scale renormalization outlined in Section
4.2.1
by induction. We first constitute the induction base.

Claim 4.10

(Induction base)
.

Consider a supercritical KSRG under the setting as in Proposition
4.1
and consider Definition
4.8
.
There exists a constant
n_{0}^{\star}>0
such that for all
n_{0}\geq n_{0}^{\star}
in
Definition

4.8
, and all
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
,

\mathbb{P}\big({\mathcal{Q}}_{0}(x)\mbox{ is $0$-bad}\big)\leq 1/{\mathrm{e}}^{6}.

Proof.

By translation invariance, we assume w.l.o.g. that
x=0
, and abbreviate
{\mathcal{Q}}_{0}:={\mathcal{Q}}_{0}(0)
.
We use the the definition of
i
-goodness in (
4.15
) for
i=0
in
Definition

4.8
, and substitute
\rho_{0}=n_{0}^{-\delta/4}
by (
4.14
). Then,

(4.23)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big({\mathcal{Q}}_{0}\mbox{ is $0$-good}\big)=\mathbb{P}\big(\exists

\displaystyle\text{ component ${\mathcal{C}}$ in $\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{n_{0}}$: }|{\mathcal{C}}|\geq n_{0}^{1-\delta/4},

\displaystyle|{\mathcal{C}}\cap{\mathcal{V}}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{spr}}[n_{0}^{\gamma},2n_{0}^{\gamma})|\geq\delta^{\prime}n_{0}^{-\delta/4}n_{0}^{\zeta}\big).

Following
Definition

4.5
, we use the alternative KSRG construction with the
q
-thinning of the vertex set. Let
{\mathcal{G}}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{base}}_{n_{0}}
be the thinned KSRG restricted to
{\mathcal{Q}}_{0}
, which is a subgraph of
\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{n_{0}}
; let
{\mathcal{C}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}_{n_{0},\mathrm{base}}[1,n_{0}^{2/(\tau-1)})
be the largest component in the induced subgraph on the restricted marks
[1,n_{0}^{2/(\tau-1)})
in
{\mathcal{G}}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{base}}_{n_{0}}
; and let
{\mathcal{C}}_{0}\supseteq{\mathcal{C}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}_{n_{0},\mathrm{base}}[1,n_{0}^{2/(\tau-1)})
be the component in the whole
\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{n_{0}}
containing this component. We define the event that this latter component is large enough:

(4.24)

{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{large}}^{n_{0}}:=\big\{|{\mathcal{C}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}_{n_{0},\mathrm{base}}[1,n_{0}^{2/(\tau-1)})|\geq n_{0}^{1-\delta/4}\big\}.

Conditionally on
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{large}}^{n_{0}}
, the component
{\mathcal{C}}_{0}
is large enough to play the role of
{\mathcal{C}}
in (
4.23
). So, by the law of total probability,

(4.25)

\mathbb{P}\big({\mathcal{Q}}_{0}\mbox{ is $0$-good}\big)\geq\mathbb{P}\big({\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{large}}^{n_{0}}\big)\cdot\mathbb{P}\big(|{\mathcal{C}}_{0}\cap{\mathcal{V}}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{spr}}_{{\mathcal{Q}}_{0}}[n_{0}^{\gamma},2n_{0}^{\gamma})|\geq\delta^{\prime}n_{0}^{-\delta/4}n_{0}^{\zeta}\mid{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{large}}^{n_{0}}\big).

We show that both factors on the right-hand side tend to one as
n_{0}\to\infty
.

(Con)-step of level-
0
.

We first consider
\mathbb{P}({\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{large}}^{n_{0}})
with the event defined in (
4.24
). If the thinned PPP
{\mathcal{V}}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{base}}_{n_{0}}
contains no vertices of mark at least
n_{0}^{2/(\tau-1)}
, then
{\mathcal{C}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}_{n_{0},\mathrm{base}}[1,n_{0}^{2/(\tau-1)})
and
{\mathcal{C}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}_{n_{0},\mathrm{base}}
coincide. Here
{\mathcal{C}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}_{n_{0},\mathrm{base}}
is the largest component of the thinned PPP in
{\mathcal{Q}}_{0}
. By a union bound over the complementary events, it follows that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}({\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{large}}^{n_{0}})

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{P}\big(|{\mathcal{V}}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{base}}_{{\mathcal{Q}}_{0}}[n_{0}^{2/(\tau-1)},\infty)|=0,\ |{\mathcal{C}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}_{n_{0},\mathrm{base}}|\geq n_{0}^{1-\delta/4},\big)

(4.26)

\displaystyle\geq 1-\mathbb{P}\big(|{\mathcal{C}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}_{n_{0},\mathrm{base}}|\leq n_{0}^{1-\delta/4}\big)-\mathbb{P}\big(|{\mathcal{V}}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{base}}_{{\mathcal{Q}}_{0}}[n_{0}^{2/(\tau-1)},\infty)|>0\big).

By
Observation

4.6
,
{\mathcal{C}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}_{n_{0},\mathrm{base}}
has the same distribution as the largest component of a supercritical KSRG with edge density
\beta q^{d}>\beta_{c}
inside the box
\Lambda_{qn_{0}}
, with
q\in(0,1)
from
Definition

4.5
. Hence, by
Lemma

4.2
, the first probability on the right-hand side in (
4.26
) tends to zero as
n_{0}q\to\infty
(we absorb the factor
q
into the exponent
1-\delta/4
by choosing
\varepsilon<\delta/4
in
Lemma

4.2
). The intensity of
{\mathcal{V}}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{base}}
is
q\mathrm{Leb}\times F_{W}(\mathrm{d}w)=q\mathrm{Leb}\times(\tau-1)w^{-\tau}\mathrm{d}w
and the box has volume
n_{0}
. So, the expected number of vertices in
{\mathcal{V}}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{base}}_{{\mathcal{Q}}_{0}}[n_{0}^{2/(\tau-1)},\infty)
is
\Theta(n_{0}^{1}n_{0}^{-2})
, which tends to zero. Hence, the second error term in (
4.26
) also tends to zero.
This shows that
\mathbb{P}({\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{large}}^{n_{0}})\to 1
in (
4.25
).

(Mark) step of level-
0
.

We now carry out the
(Mark)
-step of level
0
: We inspect the second factor in (
4.25
).
The vertices
{\mathcal{V}}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{spr}}_{{\mathcal{Q}}_{0}}[n_{0}^{\gamma},2n_{0}^{\gamma})
form a PPP with intensity
(1-q)\mathrm{Leb}\times(\tau-1)w^{-\tau}\mathrm{d}w
in a box with volume
n_{0}
. Its expected size is

(4.27)

\mathbb{E}\big[|{\mathcal{V}}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{spr}}_{{\mathcal{Q}}_{0}}[n_{0}^{\gamma},2n_{0}^{\gamma})|\big]=(1-q)(1-2^{-(\tau-1)})n_{0}^{1-\gamma(\tau-1)}=(1-q)(1-2^{-(\tau-1)})n_{0}^{\zeta}.

Further,
{\mathcal{V}}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{spr}}
is independent of
{\mathcal{V}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{base}}}
and thus also of the graph
{\mathcal{G}}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{base}}_{n_{0}}[1,n_{0}^{2/(\tau-1)})
, the graph induced by vertices whose marks are in the interval
[1,n_{0}^{2/(\tau-1)}
) and the event
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{large}}^{n_{0}}
.
On this event, there are at least
n_{0}^{1-\delta/4}
vertices in the above mentioned component, and each vertex there has mark at least one. We use that the maximal distance between two vertices in
{\mathcal{Q}}_{0}
is
\sqrt{d}n_{0}^{1/d}
. Given
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{large}}^{n_{0}}
, for each vertex
v
in
{\mathcal{V}}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{spr}}_{{\mathcal{Q}}_{0}}[n_{0}^{\gamma},2n_{0}^{\gamma})
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big(v\sim{\mathcal{C}}_{n_{0},{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{base}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}[1,n_{0}^{2/(\tau-1)})\mid{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{large}}^{n_{0}}\Big)

\displaystyle\geq 1-\Big(1-p\big(1\wedge\frac{\beta n_{0}^{\gamma}}{d^{d/2}n_{0}}\big)^{\alpha}\Big)^{n_{0}^{1-\delta/4}}

\displaystyle\geq 1-\exp\big(-p\beta^{\alpha}d^{-\alpha d/2}n_{0}^{-\delta/4}n_{0}^{\gamma\alpha-(\alpha-1)}\big)

\displaystyle=1-\exp\big(-p\beta^{\alpha}d^{-\alpha d/2}n_{0}^{-\delta/4}\big).

We used that
\gamma<1
, so
n_{0}^{\gamma-1}=o(1)
and the minimum is attained at the second term for
n_{0}
sufficiently large to obtain the first inequality. Then we used that
\gamma=(\alpha-1)/\alpha
to obtain the second row.
For
n_{0}
sufficiently large, we use the bound
1-{\mathrm{e}}^{-x}\geq x/2
for
x<1
. Therefore, the right-hand side is at least

p\beta^{\alpha}d^{-\alpha d/2}n_{0}^{-\delta/4}/2
. This is the connectivity estimate for a single vertex.
Since each vertex in
{\mathcal{V}}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{spr}}_{{\mathcal{Q}}_{0}}[n_{0}^{\gamma},2n_{0}^{\gamma})
connects independently to
{\mathcal{C}}_{n_{0},\mathrm{base}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}[1,n_{0}^{2/(\tau-1)})
, the number of connecting vertices is Poisson with mean at least the mean in (
4.27
) multiplied with this connectivity estimate. So, using
\rho_{0}=n_{0}^{-\delta/4}
in (
4.14
),

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(

\displaystyle|{\mathcal{V}}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{spr}}_{{\mathcal{Q}}_{0}}[n_{0}^{\gamma},2n_{0}^{\gamma})\cap{\mathcal{C}}_{0}|<\delta^{\prime}\rho_{0}n_{0}^{\zeta}\mid{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{large}}^{n_{0}}\big)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\big(\mathrm{Poi}\big((1-q)(1-2^{-(\tau-1)})\cdot\tfrac{1}{2}p\beta^{\alpha}d^{-\alpha d/2}\cdot n_{0}^{\zeta-\delta/4}\big)<\delta^{\prime}n_{0}^{\zeta-\delta/4}\big).

By the choice of
\delta^{\prime}
in (
4.10
) and concentration of Poisson variables in
Lemma

A.1
, the right-hand side tends to zero as
n_{0}\to\infty
. This proves that the second factor in (
4.25
) tends to
1
as well. This finishes the proof of
Claim

4.10
.
∎

4.2.4.
Advancing the induction: the iterative step.

Claim

4.10
shows that level-
0
boxes are good with constant probability. In the next claim we advance the induction by bounding the probabilities of the steps
(Sub), (Con), (Mark)
in Section
4.2.1
for level-
i
boxes.

Claim 4.11

(Advancing the induction)
.

Consider a supercritical KSRG on a PPP with parameters such that
\zeta_{\mathrm{hl}}>0
. Assume
\delta\in(0,\zeta/2)
.
There exists a constant
n_{0}^{\star}>0
such that for all
n_{0}\geq n_{0}^{\star}
in
Definition

4.8
, for all
i\geq 0
,

(4.28)

\mathbb{P}\big({\mathcal{Q}}_{i}(x)\mbox{ is $i$-bad}\big)\leq\exp\big(-6(n_{i}/n_{0})^{\zeta-\delta}\big):=\mathrm{err}_{i}.

Proof.

When
i=0
the statement follows from
Claim

4.10
.
Let
i\geq 1
and assume that (
4.28
) holds for level-
(i-1)
boxes. We now follow the steps
(Sub), (Con), (Mark)
in Section
4.2.1
and bound their error probabilities. Like in
Claim

4.10
, we assume w.l.o.g. that
x=0
, and write
{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}:={\mathcal{Q}}_{i}(0)
.

Enough subboxes are good.

We start with the
(Sub)
step.
Since
n_{i}=m_{i}n_{i-1}
by (
4.11
), we can partition
{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}(x)
into
m_{i}
subboxes of volume
n_{i-1}
. Let
(x_{i})_{i\leq m_{i}}
denote the centers of these boxes. Define the event

(4.29)

{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{sub}}:=\Big\{\sum_{j\in[m_{i}]}\mathds{1}_{\{{\mathcal{Q}}_{i-1}(x_{j})\text{ is $(i-1)$-bad}\}}<\varepsilon_{i}m_{i}\Big\}.

In words,
{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}(x)
contains at least
(1-\varepsilon_{i})m_{i}
many
(i-1)
-good subboxes.
Using that disjoint boxes are i.i.d., the induction hypothesis yields that the number of bad boxes is stochastically dominated by a
\mathrm{Bin}(m_{i},\mathrm{err}_{i-1})
random variable, i.e.,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(\neg{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{sub}})\leq\mathbb{P}\Big(\mathrm{Bin}\big(m_{i},\mathrm{err}_{i-1})\big)\geq\varepsilon_{i}m_{i}\Big)\leq\sum_{k=\lceil\varepsilon_{i}m_{i}\rceil}^{m_{i}}\binom{m_{i}}{k}\mathrm{err}_{i-1}^{k}.

We use that
\binom{m_{i}}{k}\leq({\mathrm{e}}m_{i}/k)^{k}
, so the binomial coefficient is at most
({\mathrm{e}}/\varepsilon_{i})^{k}
for all
k\geq\varepsilon_{i}m_{i}
.
Hence, using the definition of
\mathrm{err}_{i-1}
from (
4.28
),

\mathbb{P}\big(\neg{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{sub}})\leq\sum_{k=\lceil\varepsilon_{i}m_{i}\rceil}^{\infty}\exp\big(-k(6(n_{i-1}/n_{0})^{\zeta-\delta}-\log({\mathrm{e}}/\varepsilon_{i}))\big).

We use that
\varepsilon_{i}=(i+1)^{-2}
by definition in (
4.13
): when
i=1
we compute
6-\log({\mathrm{e}}/\varepsilon_{1})=6-\log(4{\mathrm{e}})=5-\log(4)>3
; for
i\geq 2
, by the second inequality in (
4.20
) for
C=4/6
, we obtain that
6(n_{i-1}/n_{0})^{\zeta-\delta}-\log({\mathrm{e}}/\varepsilon_{i})>3(n_{i-1}/n_{0})^{\zeta-\delta}
when
n_{0}
is sufficiently large.
We obtain a geometric series with base
\exp(-3(n_{i-1}/n_{0})^{\zeta-\delta})
, which is at most
1/2
for all
i\geq 2
by choosing
n_{0}
sufficiently large. Evaluating the geometric sum and then using the bound (Sub) in (
4.16
) yields

(4.30)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(\neg{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{sub}})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=\lceil\varepsilon_{i}m_{i}\rceil}^{\infty}\exp\big(-3(n_{i-1}/n_{0})^{\zeta-\delta}\big)^{k}\leq\exp\big(-3\varepsilon_{i}m_{i}(n_{i-1}/n_{0})^{\zeta-\delta}\big)/2

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{1}{3}\exp\big(-6(n_{i}/n_{0})^{\zeta-\delta}\big).

This finishes the
(Sub)
-step of level-
i
.

Connectivity: the local giant is big enough.

We now move on to the
(Con)
-step in Section
4.2.1
.
In level
i-1
, we used the vertex sets
{\mathcal{V}}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{base}}_{{\mathcal{Q}}_{i-1}(x_{j})}\big[1,n_{0}^{2/(\tau-1)}\big)\cup{\mathcal{V}}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{spr}}_{{\mathcal{Q}}_{i-1}(x_{j})}[1,2n_{i-1}^{\gamma})
in each subbox
j\in[m_{i}]
, see (
4.12
). We denote the level-
(i-1)
graphs on these vertex sets by
\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{n_{i-1},x_{j}}
.
We now reveal the realization of
(\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{n_{i-1},x_{j}})_{j\leq m_{i}}
for all
j\leq m_{i}
in the level-
(i-1)
subboxes of
{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}(x)
, and assume that the realization satisfies the event
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{sub}}
for
{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}(x)
.
We write

(4.31)

\displaystyle\mathrm{pre\textnormal{-}}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{n_{i}}

\displaystyle:=\cup_{j\leq m_{i}}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{n_{i-1},x_{j}}\quad\mbox{on}\quad

\displaystyle{\mathcal{V}}_{\mathrm{pre}}

\displaystyle:=\cup_{j\leq m_{i}}{\mathcal{V}}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{base}}_{{\mathcal{Q}}_{i-1}(x_{j})}\big[1,n_{0}^{2/(\tau-1)}\big)\cup{\mathcal{V}}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{spr}}_{{\mathcal{Q}}_{i-1}(x_{j})}[1,2n_{i-1}^{\gamma}).

Since the realization satisfies
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{sub}}
, at least
(1-\varepsilon_{i})m_{i}
many subboxes are
(i-1)
-good.
We re-label boxes so that the boxes with indices
j\leq(1-\varepsilon_{i})m_{i}
are all
(i-1)
-good. In
\mathrm{pre\textnormal{-}}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{n_{i}}
we did not reveal yet whether vertices in
different
level-
(i-1)
subboxes are connected to each other or not. The graph
\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{n_{i}}
which contains
\mathrm{pre\textnormal{-}}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{n_{i}}

additionally contains these edges between subboxes.

The Erdős-Rényi type auxiliary graph.
The definition of
(i-1)
-goodness in (
4.15
) gives us components
{\mathcal{C}}_{i-1,x_{j}}
in each good subbox with size at least
\rho_{i-1}n_{i-1}
. Consider the auxiliary graph
{\mathcal{H}}=({\mathcal{V}}_{\mathcal{H}},{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathcal{H}})
in which the vertex
j\in{\mathcal{V}}_{\mathcal{H}}:=[\lceil(1-\varepsilon_{i})m_{i}\rceil]
corresponds to
{\mathcal{C}}_{i-1,x_{j}}
, and two vertices
j,k\in{\mathcal{V}}_{\mathcal{H}}
are connected by an edge if the components
{\mathcal{C}}_{i-1,x_{j}}
and
{\mathcal{C}}_{i-1,x_{k}}
are connected by an edge in
\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{n_{i}}
. If the auxiliary graph
{\mathcal{H}}
is connected, then
{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}(x)
contains a component that has size at least
(1-\varepsilon_{i})m_{i}\cdot\rho_{i-1}n_{i-1}
. Since
m_{i}n_{i-1}=n_{i}
and
\rho_{i-1}(1-\varepsilon_{i})=\rho_{i}
by (
4.11
) and (
4.14
), respectively, this size is at least
\rho_{i}n_{i}
.
Therefore, it satisfies the first criterion for being
i
-good in (
4.15
).
Hence, for all realizations
\mathrm{pre\textnormal{-}}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{n_{i}}
satisfying
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{sub}}
,

(4.32)

\mathbb{P}(\exists\mbox{ component }{\mathcal{C}}_{i}:|{\mathcal{C}}_{i}|\geq\rho_{i}n_{i}\mid\mathrm{pre\textnormal{-}}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{n_{i}})\geq\mathbb{P}({\mathcal{H}}\mbox{ connected}\mid\mathrm{pre\textnormal{-}}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{n_{i}}).

We now prove that
{\mathcal{H}}
stochastically dominates an Erdős-Rényi graph. The criteria for being
(i-1)
-good in (
4.15
) guarantee that the level-
(i-1)
good subboxes contain a component
{\mathcal{C}}_{i-1,x_{j}}
with size at least
\rho_{i-1}n_{i-1}
,
and
this component contains at least
\delta^{\prime}\rho_{i-1}n_{i-1}^{\zeta}
vertices of mark in
[n_{i-1}^{\gamma},2n_{i-1}^{\gamma})
.
We bound the probability that there is an edge between two such components in different good subboxes by checking whether any of the potential edges between the
\delta^{\prime}\rho_{i-1}n_{i-1}^{\zeta}
many level-
(i-1)
high mark vertices in one box and the
\rho_{i-1}n_{i-1}
many vertices in the component of mark at least
1
in the other box is present.
Any two vertices in
Q_{i}(x)
are at distance at most
\sqrt{d}n_{i}^{1/d}
, so using the connection probability in (
4.6
) we compute for any
good
subboxes with index
j,k
that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big({\mathcal{C}}_{i-1,x_{j}}\nsim_{\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{n_{i}}}{\mathcal{C}}_{i-1,x_{k}}\mid\text{pre-}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{n_{i}}\big)

\displaystyle\leq\Big(1-p\big(1\wedge\tfrac{\beta n_{i-1}^{\gamma}}{\sqrt{d}^{d}n_{i}}\big)^{\alpha}\Big)^{\delta^{\prime}\rho_{i-1}^{2}n_{i-1}^{1+\zeta}}

\displaystyle\leq\exp\big(-p\delta^{\prime}\beta^{\alpha}d^{-\alpha d/2}\rho_{i-1}^{2}n_{i-1}^{1+\zeta+\gamma\alpha}n_{i}^{-\alpha}\big)

(4.33)

\displaystyle=\exp\big(-p\delta^{\prime}\beta^{\alpha}d^{-\alpha d/2}\rho_{i-1}^{2}n_{i-1}^{\zeta+\alpha}n_{i}^{-\alpha}\big)=:1-q_{i},

where we used that
\gamma=1-1/\alpha
to get the last row, and also to see that the minimum is at the second term in the first row, since
n_{i-1}^{\gamma}/n_{i}
can be made arbitrarily small by choosing
n_{0}
sufficiently large, see (
4.11
). The connectivity between different local large components is conditionally independent given the realization of
\mathrm{pre}\textnormal{-}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{n_{i}}
, so the auxiliary graph
{\mathcal{H}}
dominates an Erdős-Rényi graph
G(\lceil(1-\varepsilon_{i})m_{i}\rceil,q_{i})
.
The reader may verify that
m_{i}q_{i}\gg\log m_{i}
for all
i\geq 1
if
n_{0}
is sufficiently large, so by
Claim

4.7
on the connectivity of
G
,

\displaystyle\mathds{1}_{\{{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{sub}}\}}\mathbb{P}\big({\mathcal{H}}\text{ not conn.}\mid\text{pre-}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{n_{i}}\big)

\displaystyle\leq 3({\mathrm{e}}m_{i})^{2}(1-q_{i})^{(1-\varepsilon_{i})m_{i}/2}

(4.34)

\displaystyle\leq 3({\mathrm{e}}m_{i})^{2}\exp\big(-\tfrac{3p\delta^{\prime}\beta^{\alpha}}{8d^{\alpha d/2}}\rho_{i-1}^{2}n_{i-1}^{\zeta+\alpha}n_{i}^{-\alpha}m_{i}\big),

where we substituted
1-q_{i}
from (
4.33
), and used that
1-\varepsilon_{i}\geq 3/4
for all
i\geq 1
.
We substitute
n_{i}=m_{i}n_{i-1}
to obtain the first row below, and then use the inequality (Con) in (
4.17
) to see that the
i
-dependent factors together can be bounded from below by
Cn_{i}^{\zeta-\delta}
for any constant
C>0
to get the second row. Here, we can choose
n_{0}
sufficiently large so that
C
cancels out the constant (
\delta^{\prime},\beta,p,d
-dependent) prefactors:

\displaystyle\mathds{1}_{\{{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{sub}}\}}\mathbb{P}\big({\mathcal{H}}\text{ not conn.}\mid\text{pre-}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{n_{i}}\big)

\displaystyle\leq 3({\mathrm{e}}m_{i})^{2}\exp\big(-\tfrac{3p\delta^{\prime}\beta^{\alpha}}{8d^{\alpha d/2}}\rho_{i-1}^{2}n_{i-1}^{\zeta}m_{i}^{1-\alpha}\big).

(4.35)

\displaystyle\leq 3({\mathrm{e}}m_{i})^{2}\exp\big(-7n_{i}^{\zeta-\delta}\big)\leq\tfrac{1}{3}\exp\big(-6n_{i}^{\zeta-\delta}\big).

Returning to (
4.32
), this shows that the first condition of being level-
i
good in (
4.15
), i.e., containing a connected component with size at least
\rho_{i}n_{i}
, holds with probability at least
1-\exp(-6n_{i}^{\zeta-\delta})/3
, given
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{sub}}
. This finishes the
(Con)
-step. We summarize it as follows:
since
\mathbb{P}\big(\neg{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{sub}})\leq\exp\big(-6(n_{i}/n_{0})^{\zeta-\delta}\big)/3
by (
4.30
),

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big({\mathcal{Q}}_{i}(x)\text{ is $i$-bad}\big)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\big(\neg{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{sub}}\big)+\mathbb{E}\big[\mathds{1}_{\{{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{sub}}\}}\mathbb{P}\big({\mathcal{Q}}_{i}(x)\text{ is $i$-bad}\mid\text{pre-}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{n_{i}}\big)\big]

(4.36)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\leq\mathbb{P}\big(\neg{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{sub}}\big)+\mathbb{E}\big[\mathds{1}_{\{{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{sub}}\}}\mathbb{P}\big({\mathcal{H}}\text{ not conn.}\mid\text{pre-}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{n_{i}}\big)\big]\\
&\quad+\mathbb{E}\big[\mathds{1}_{\{{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{sub}}\}}\mathbb{P}\big({\mathcal{Q}}_{i}(x)\text{ is $i$-bad}\mid\text{pre-}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{n_{i}},{\mathcal{H}}\text{ connected}\big)\big]\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{2}{3}\exp\big(-6(n_{i}/n_{0})^{\zeta-\delta}\big)+\mathbb{E}\big[\mathds{1}_{\{{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{sub}}\}}\mathbb{P}\big({\mathcal{Q}}_{i}(x)\text{ is $i$-bad}\mid\text{pre-}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{n_{i}},{\mathcal{H}}\text{ conn.}\big)\big],

where the error of the
(Con)
-step is the first term in the last row, and the second term still needs to be bounded during the
(Mark)
step.

Enough high-mark vertices in level-
i
.

Finally, we treat the
(Mark)
-step in Section
4.2.1
. That is, we bound the conditional probability in the last row above. This corresponds to the second criterion in (
4.15
), i.e., the requirement that
{\mathcal{C}}_{i}
also contains enough level-
i
high mark vertices.
On the event
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{sub}}\cap\{{\mathcal{H}}\text{ is connected}\}
,
we set
{\mathcal{C}}_{i}
to be the connected component in
\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{n_{i}}
that contains
\cup_{j\in{\mathcal{V}}_{\mathcal{H}}}{\mathcal{C}}_{i-1,x_{j}}=:\mathrm{pre\textnormal{-}}{\mathcal{C}}_{i}
. By the argument leading to (
4.32
),
\mathrm{pre\textnormal{-}}{\mathcal{C}}_{i}
has already size at least
\rho_{i}n_{i}
.
Hence, by the criterion in (
4.15
), we need to bound

(4.37)

\displaystyle\mathds{1}_{\{{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{sub}}\}}\mathbb{P}\big(|{\mathcal{V}}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{spr}}_{{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}(x)}[n_{i}^{\gamma},2n_{i}^{\gamma})\cap{\mathcal{C}}_{i}|<\delta^{\prime}\rho_{i}n_{i}^{1-\gamma(\tau-1)}

\displaystyle\mid\text{pre-}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{n_{i}},{\mathcal{H}}\text{ connected}\big).

We argue similarly as for
i=0
around and below (
4.27
). Since
\mathrm{pre\textnormal{-}}{\mathcal{C}}_{i}\subseteq{\mathcal{V}}_{\mathrm{pre}}
in (
4.31
), and
2n_{i-1}^{\gamma}<n_{i}^{\gamma}
by (
4.22
), the already revealed vertex set
{\mathcal{V}}_{\mathrm{pre}}
is disjoint and thus independent of
{\mathcal{V}}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{spr}}_{{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}(x)}[n_{i}^{\gamma},2n_{i}^{\gamma})
.
Hence, the PPP
{\mathcal{V}}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{spr}}_{{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}(x)}[n_{i}^{\gamma},2n_{i}^{\gamma})
itself is independent of the conditioning in (
4.37
). Using the intensity in (
4.5
), its expected size is

(4.38)

\mathbb{E}\big[|{\mathcal{V}}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{spr}}_{{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}(x)}[n_{i}^{\gamma},2n_{i}^{\gamma})|\big]=(1-q)(1-2^{-(\tau-1)})n_{i}^{1-\gamma(\tau-1)}=(1-q)(1-2^{-(\tau-1)})n_{i}^{\zeta},

with
q\in(0,1)
from the
Definition

4.5
. There are at least
\rho_{i}n_{i}
vertices in
\mathrm{pre\textnormal{-}}{\mathcal{C}}_{i}
, each with mark at least one, and each pair of vertices inside
{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}
is within distance
\sqrt{d}n_{i}^{1/d}
. So, we get using the connection probability in (
4.6
) that each individual vertex in
{\mathcal{V}}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{spr}}_{{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}(x)}[n_{i}^{\gamma},2n_{i}^{\gamma})
connects by an edge to
\mathrm{pre\textnormal{-}}{\mathcal{C}}_{i}\subseteq{\mathcal{C}}_{i}
with probability at least

\displaystyle 1-\Big(1-p\big(1\wedge\tfrac{\beta n_{i}^{\gamma}}{\sqrt{d}^{d}n_{i}}\big)^{\alpha}\Big)^{\rho_{i}n_{i}}

\displaystyle\geq 1-\exp\big(-p\beta^{\alpha}d^{-\alpha d/2}\rho_{i}n_{i}^{\gamma\alpha-(\alpha-1)}\big)

\displaystyle=1-\exp\big(-p\beta^{\alpha}d^{-\alpha d/2}\rho_{i}\big).

To obtain the second expression, we used that the minimum is at the second term for
n_{0}
sufficiently large, and
\gamma=(\alpha-1)/\alpha
to get the second row.
Since
\rho_{i}=\Theta(n_{0}^{-\delta/4})
as
n_{0}
tends to infinity by (
4.14
), we may assume that
n_{0}
is so large so that the bound
1-e^{-x}\geq x/2
applies (for all
x\leq 1
), and the right-hand side is at least

p\beta^{\alpha}d^{-\alpha d/2}\rho_{i}/2
.
Since each vertex in
{\mathcal{V}}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{spr}}_{{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}(x)}[n_{i}^{\gamma},2n_{i}^{\gamma})
connects independently to
\mathrm{pre\textnormal{-}}{\mathcal{C}}_{i}
, the number of connecting vertices forms a PPP with mean at least the product of (
4.38
) multiplied with this bound. So,

\displaystyle\mathds{1}_{\{{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{sub}}\}}\mathbb{P}\big(

\displaystyle|{\mathcal{V}}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{spr}}_{{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}(x)}[n_{i}^{\gamma},2n_{i}^{\gamma})\cap{\mathcal{C}}_{i}|<\delta^{\prime}\rho_{i}n_{i}^{\zeta}\mid\text{pre-}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{n_{i}},{\mathcal{H}}\text{ conn.}\big)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\big(\mathrm{Poi}\big((1-q)(1-2^{-(\tau-1)})\cdot\tfrac{1}{2}p\beta^{\alpha}d^{-\alpha d/2}\cdot\rho_{i}n_{i}^{\zeta}\big)<\delta^{\prime}\rho_{i}n_{i}^{\zeta}\big).

The value of
\delta^{\prime}
in (
4.10
) is chosen exactly so that
2\delta^{\prime}
is the prefactor of
\rho_{i}n_{i}^{\zeta}
in the mean of the Poisson variable. Hence,
by the concentration of Poisson variables in
Lemma

A.1
, there exists
c=c(\delta^{\prime})>0
such that

\mathds{1}_{\{{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{sub}}\}}\mathbb{P}\big(|{\mathcal{V}}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{spr}}_{{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}(x)}[n_{i}^{\gamma},2n_{i}^{\gamma})\cap{\mathcal{C}}_{i}|<\delta^{\prime}\rho_{i}n_{i}^{\zeta}\mid\text{pre-}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{n_{i}},{\mathcal{H}}\text{ conn.}\big)<\exp\big(-c\rho_{i}n_{i}^{\zeta}\big),

Finally, by the bound (Mark) in (
4.18
), the right-hand side is at most
\exp\big(-6n_{i}^{\zeta-\delta}\big)/3
for
n_{0}
sufficiently large. Combining this bound with (
4.36
) finishes the proof of
Claim

4.11
.
∎

If the box
{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}(x)
is
i
-good, then it contains a giant component on the restricted marks
[1,2n_{i}^{\gamma})
. Thus, Claims
4.10
and
4.11
together prove Proposition
4.1

along the subsequence

n_{i}
, and when
\zeta=\zeta_{\mathrm{hl}}
. We now prove Proposition
4.1
for arbitrary
n
.

Proof of Proposition
4.1

when
\zeta_{\mathrm{hl}}>0
.

W.l.o.g. we may assume that
\delta\in(0,\zeta/2\wedge 1-\zeta)
in the statement of the lemma. Let
n_{0}
be a constant such that Claims
4.10
–
4.11
hold for all
i\geq 0
.
Fix now the sequence
n_{i}
in (
4.11
).
We may assume that
n>n_{0}
, since the constant
A
in the statement of Proposition
4.1
can be increased to account for values
n\leq n_{0}
. We
define

(4.39)

\displaystyle i_{0}

\displaystyle:=\min\{i:2n_{i}^{\gamma}>n_{0}^{2/(\tau-1)}\},

\displaystyle i_{\ast}

\displaystyle:=\max\{i:n_{i}<n\},

\displaystyle m

\displaystyle:=\lfloor(n/n_{i_{\ast}})^{1/d}\rfloor^{d},

\displaystyle\tilde{n}

\displaystyle:=n_{i_{\ast}}m.

By increasing the constant
A
in the statement of Proposition
4.1
, we may assume w.l.o.g. that
i_{\ast}>i_{0}
.
Then, there exist
\nu
such that

(4.40)

m\geq\nu(n/n_{i_{\ast}}).

By the definition of
m
and
\tilde{n}
,
\Lambda_{\tilde{n}}\subseteq\Lambda_{n}
can be partitioned into exactly
m\geq 1
disjoint boxes of volume
n_{i_{\ast}}
. We denote the boxes of this partition by
{\mathcal{Q}}_{i_{\ast}}(x_{1}),\ldots,{\mathcal{Q}}_{i_{\ast}}(x_{m})
. From here on we argue similarly as in the proof of
Claim

4.11
: we use
(Sub)
and
Con
-type steps.
We will show that at least half of the boxes are
i_{\ast}
-good, and afterwards connect the large components in the first
\lceil m/2\rceil
many
i_{\ast}
-good boxes via a domination to an Erdős-Rényi random graph.

Enough subboxes are good.

Define

{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{sub}}(n):=\bigg\{\sum_{j\in[m]}\mathds{1}_{\{{\mathcal{Q}}_{i_{\ast}}(x_{j})\text{ is $i_{\ast}$-bad}\}}<m/2\bigg\}.

We follow the calculations from (
4.29
) until (
4.30
) and replace
\varepsilon_{i}
by
1/2
,
m_{i}
by
m
and
n_{i-1}
by
n_{i_{\ast}}
there. The bound
{m\choose k}\leq(m/{\mathrm{e}}k)^{k}
works for all
m\geq 1
and
k\leq m
. We also use there the second inequality in (
4.20
) for
C=4/6
, which also holds for
n_{i_{\ast}}
. So all bounds except the last row in (
4.30
) remain valid. Using (
4.40
) we obtain the bound

(4.41)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(\neg{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{sub}}(n))

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=\lceil m/2\rceil}^{\infty}\exp\big(-3(n_{i_{\ast}}/n_{0})^{\zeta-\delta}\big)^{k}\leq\exp\big(-3(m/2)(n_{i_{\ast}}/n_{0})^{\zeta-\delta}\big)/2

\displaystyle\leq\exp\Big(-(\nu/2n_{0}^{\zeta-\delta})\cdot nn_{i_{\ast}}^{\zeta-\delta-1}\Big).

As
n>n_{i_{\ast}}
and
\zeta<1
, it follows that
n_{i_{\ast}}^{\zeta-\delta-1}>n^{\zeta-\delta-1}
, which, when rearranged yields that
nn_{i_{\ast}}^{\zeta-\delta-1}>n^{\zeta-\delta}
. Hence,

(4.42)

\mathbb{P}\big(\neg{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{sub}}(n)\big)\leq\exp\Big(-(\nu/2n_{0}^{\zeta-\delta})\cdot n^{\zeta-\delta}\Big).

This finishes the
(Sub)
-step for general
n
.

Connecting local-giants of subboxes.
Now we move on to the
(Con)
-step in Section
4.2.1
. We follow the argument around (
4.31
), and define the pre-vertex set
{\mathcal{V}}_{\mathrm{pre}}
as there, with
n_{i}
replaced by
n
,
i-1
replaced by
i_{\ast}
, and
m_{i}
replaced by
m
there:

(4.43)

\displaystyle\mathrm{pre\textnormal{-}}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{\tilde{n}}

\displaystyle:=\cup_{j\leq m}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{n_{i_{\ast}},x_{j}}\quad\mbox{on}\quad

\displaystyle{\mathcal{V}}_{\mathrm{pre}}(\tilde{n})

\displaystyle:=\cup_{j\leq m}{\mathcal{V}}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{base}}_{{\mathcal{Q}}_{i_{\ast}}(x_{j})}\big[1,n_{0}^{2/(\tau-1)}\big)\cup{\mathcal{V}}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{spr}}_{{\mathcal{Q}}_{i_{\ast}}(x_{j})}[1,2n_{i_{\ast}}^{\gamma}).

We reveal the realization of
\mathrm{pre\textnormal{-}}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{n}
, satisfying the
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{sub}}(n)
. Following the argument above (
4.32
), we construct the auxiliary graph
{\mathcal{H}}_{n}
on vertices representing the first
[\lceil m/2\rceil]
many
i_{\ast}
-good subboxes. Each vertex
j
represents a large component
{\mathcal{C}}_{i_{\ast},x_{j}}
guaranteed by the first condition in
i
-goodness in (
4.15
). Two vertices
j,k
are connected by an edge in
{\mathcal{H}}_{n}
if there is an edge between
{\mathcal{C}}_{i_{\ast},x_{j}}
and
{\mathcal{C}}_{i_{\ast},x_{k}}
. We follow the computations between (
4.32
) and (
4.34
), and replace there
(1-\varepsilon_{i})m_{i}
with
m/2
, replace
n_{i-1},\rho_{i-1}
with
n_{i_{\ast}},\rho_{i_{\ast}}
, and replace
n_{i}
by
\tilde{n}
. The latter holds because
mn_{i_{\ast}}=\tilde{n}
by (
4.39
). Then, for some constant
c>0
, (
4.34
) turns into

\displaystyle\mathds{1}_{\{{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{sub}}(n)\}}\mathbb{P}\big({\mathcal{H}}_{n}\text{ not conn.}\mid\text{pre-}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{\tilde{n}}\big)

\displaystyle\leq A^{\prime}m\exp\big(-c\rho_{i_{\ast}}^{2}n_{i_{\ast}}^{\zeta+\alpha}\tilde{n}^{-\alpha}m\big).

By (
4.39
),
m\leq m_{i_{\ast}+1}
, so the bound (
4.21
) holds for
\tilde{n}=mn_{i_{\ast}}
, which gives the first row below. Then,
\tilde{n}\geq\nu n
follows by multiplying both sides of (
4.40
). Moreover, the prefactor
A^{\prime}m<A^{\prime}n
can be absorbed by decreasing the constant prefactor in the exponent. We obtain for some
c^{\prime}>c^{\prime\prime}>0

\displaystyle\mathds{1}_{\{{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{sub}}(n)\}}\mathbb{P}\big({\mathcal{H}}_{n}\text{ not connected}\mid\text{pre-}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{\tilde{n}}\big)

\displaystyle\leq A^{\prime}m\exp\big(-c^{\prime}\rho_{i_{\ast}}^{2}\tilde{n}^{\zeta-\delta}\big)

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\exp\big(-c^{\prime\prime}\rho_{i_{\ast}}^{2}n^{\zeta-\delta}\big).

The definition of
\rho_{i}
in (
4.14
) gives that
\rho_{i_{\ast}}>\rho>0
for all
i_{\ast}
, which gives our desired bound for the
(Con)
-step. Combining the error bound in (
4.42
) with this, we obtain that for any
A>0
sufficiently large that only depends on the fixed value of the constant
n_{0}
,

\mathbb{P}\big({\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{sub}}(n)\cap\{{\mathcal{H}}_{n}\mbox{ connected}\}\big)\geq 1-\exp\big(-\tfrac{1}{A}n^{\zeta-\delta}\big).

By (
4.43
) and the arguments below, connectivity of the auxiliary graph
{\mathcal{H}}_{n}
means that the large components
{\mathcal{C}}_{i_{\ast},x_{j}}
in the first
m/2

i_{\ast}
-good boxes form a connected graph. In pre-
\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{\tilde{n}}
we only revealed edges between vertices of mark at most
2n_{i_{\ast}}^{\gamma}<2n^{\gamma}
by (
4.43
). By (
4.39
) and the assumption that
i_{\ast}>i_{0}
below (
4.39
), we also have
2n_{i_{\ast}}^{\gamma}>n_{0}^{2/(\tau-1)}
.
Moreover, on
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{sub}}(n)\cap\{{\mathcal{H}}_{n}\mbox{ connected}\}
, the
\lceil m/2\rceil
components of size at least
\rho_{i_{\ast}}n_{i_{\ast}}
form a connected graph, and thus
{\mathcal{G}}_{n}[1,2n_{i_{\ast}}^{\gamma})
contains a component of size at least

(m/2)\cdot\rho_{i_{\ast}}n_{i_{\ast}}=\rho_{i_{\ast}}\tilde{n}/2\geq(\nu/2)\rho_{i_{\ast}}n\geq(\nu/2)\rho n.

This implies that the largest component must be at least this large as well. So,

(4.44)

{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{sub}}(n)\cap\{{\mathcal{H}}_{n}\mbox{ connected}\}\,\subseteq\,\big\{|{\mathcal{C}}_{n}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}[1,An^{\gamma})|\geq\tfrac{1}{A}n\big\}

when
A
is sufficiently large.
This finishes the proof of (
4.1
). We do not need the
(Mark)
step here since there is no further iteration. For the Palm version
\mathbb{P}^{\scriptscriptstyle x}
the proof is exactly the same.
∎

Proof of Proposition
4.1
when
\zeta_{\mathrm{ll}}>0
.

When the vertex is formed by a PPP, we obtain the second statement (
4.2
) by a straightforward adaptation of the above proofs. We provide a sketch.
When
\zeta_{\mathrm{ll}}=2-\alpha>0
, we re-define what we call an
i
-good box by leaving the requirement on the marks in (
4.15
) out.
We now call
{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}(x)

i
-good (and
i
-bad otherwise) if it satisfies the event

(4.45)

{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{i\textnormal{-}good}}(x):=\Big\{\exists\text{ component ${\mathcal{C}}_{i}$ in }\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{n_{i}}:|{\mathcal{C}}_{i}|\geq\rho_{i}n_{i}\Big\}.

Then, we replace
\zeta_{\mathrm{hl}}
by
\zeta_{\mathrm{ll}}=2-\alpha
in all computations and definitions, and we skip the
(Mark)
-step on each level. We do this by setting the level-
i
vertex set to be
\widehat{\mathcal{V}}_{i}:={\mathcal{V}}_{{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}(x)}[1,n_{0}^{2/(\tau-1)})
. That is, only the size of the box changes across levels, the mark-truncation stays put. The
(Sub)
-step leading to (
4.30
) carries through unchanged by the inductive assumption and the concentration of binomial random variables on the number of level-
(i-1)
good subboxes. For the
(Con)
-step, instead of using high-mark vertices, we estimate the mark of each vertex from below by
1
in the large components
{\mathcal{C}}_{i-1,x_{j}}
and
{\mathcal{C}}_{i-1,x_{k}}
. Using this lower bound, the number of edges between the two components dominates a binomial random variable. Both components have size at least
\rho_{i-1}n_{i-1}
because they are contained in
(i-1)
-good boxes, and their distance is at most
\sqrt{d}n_{i}^{1/d}
, so (
4.33
) simplifies to

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big({\mathcal{C}}_{i-1,x_{j}}\nsim_{\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{n_{i}}}{\mathcal{C}}_{i-1,x_{k}}\mid\text{pre-}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{n_{i}})

\displaystyle\leq\Big(1-p\big(1\wedge\tfrac{\beta}{\sqrt{d}^{d}n_{i}}\big)^{\alpha}\Big)^{\rho_{i-1}^{2}n_{i-1}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\exp\big(-(p\beta d^{-\alpha d/2})\cdot\rho_{i-1}^{2}n_{i-1}^{2}n_{i}^{-\alpha}\big)=:1-q_{i}.

Observe that the exponent
n_{i-1}^{2}=n_{i-1}^{\zeta+\alpha}
since here
\zeta=2-\alpha
. From here on the computations are identical to those between (
4.34
) and (
4.35
), and since the last term in (
4.36
) is not present, we conclude the proof at (
4.36
) for
\zeta_{\mathrm{ll}}>0
.
Along the lines we also use that all statements of
Claim

4.9
remain valid also for
\zeta=\zeta_{\mathrm{ll}}
, except (
4.22
). But, (
4.22
) is only used in the
(Mark)
-step which we omit when we consider the case
\zeta_{\mathrm{ll}}>0
.
∎

5.
Sharp bounds on the existence of a giant

The goal of this section is to improve upon the probability of the existence of the giant component compared to Proposition
4.1
, which is formalized in the next proposition. In Proposition
4.1
, the exponent of
n
in the stretched exponential decay is
\max(\zeta_{\mathrm{hl}},\zeta_{\mathrm{ll}})-\delta
. Here we remove the error
\delta
and ‘append’
(d-1)/d,\zeta_{\mathrm{hh}}
to the list in the maximum.

For a regime
\mathrm{type}\in\{\mathrm{ll},\mathrm{hl},\mathrm{hh}\}
, and constants
\rho,\delta\in(0,1)
, we define the event

(5.1)

{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{giant}}^{\mathrm{type}}(\rho):=\begin{cases}\big\{\exists\mbox{ comp. ${\mathcal{C}}$ in ${\mathcal{G}}_{n}$}:|{\mathcal{C}}|\geq\rho n,{\mathcal{C}}\supseteq{\mathcal{V}}[n^{(1-\delta)/(\tau-1)},\infty)\big\},&\text{if }\mathrm{type}\in\{\mathrm{hl},\mathrm{hh}\},\\
\big\{\exists\mbox{ comp. ${\mathcal{C}}$ in ${\mathcal{G}}_{n}$}:|{\mathcal{C}}|\geq\rho n\big\},&\text{if }\mathrm{type}=\mathrm{ll}.\end{cases}

Recall the values
\gamma_{\mathrm{hl}}
and
\gamma_{\mathrm{hh}}
from (
2.11
) and (
2.13
), respectively. Define

(5.2)

\gamma_{\mathrm{ll}}:=(\alpha-1)/(\tau-1),

which we will only use when
\zeta_{\mathrm{ll}}=2-\alpha>0
. In particular, we will not use it when
\alpha=\infty
.

Proposition 5.1

(Sharp bounds for the giant)
.

Consider a supercritical KSRG satisfying Assumption
2.2
on a PPP or consider supercritical long-range percolation on
{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}
. Assume that there is a
\mathrm{type}\in\{\mathrm{ll},\mathrm{hl},\mathrm{hh}\}
so that
\zeta_{\mathrm{type}}>0
and that for a constant
\rho>0
,

(5.3)

\mathbb{P}\big({\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{giant}}^{\mathrm{type}}(\rho)\big)\longrightarrow 1,\qquad\text{as }n\to\infty.

(long)

Then for all
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
A>0
such that for all
n\geq 1

(5.4)

\mathbb{P}\big(|{\mathcal{C}}_{n}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}[1,An^{\gamma_{\mathrm{type}}})|<(1-\varepsilon)\rho n\big)\leq\exp\big(-\tfrac{1}{A}n^{\zeta_{\mathrm{type}}}\big).

(short)

Also, for all
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
A>0
such that for all
n\geq 1

(5.5)

\mathbb{P}\big(|{\mathcal{C}}_{n}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}[1,A)|<(1-\varepsilon)\rho n\big)\leq\exp\big(-\tfrac{1}{A}n^{\zeta_{\mathrm{short}}}\big).

Proposition

5.1
gives sharp decay for the value of
\zeta_{\mathrm{type}}
that maximizes
\{\zeta_{\mathrm{ll}},\zeta_{\mathrm{hl}},\zeta_{\mathrm{hh}},\zeta_{\mathrm{short}}\}
, which is equal to
\zeta_{\star}
by
Claim

2.3
. When
\zeta_{\mathrm{long}}=\max\{\zeta_{\mathrm{ll}},\zeta_{\mathrm{hl}},\zeta_{\mathrm{hh}}\}>0
and
(d-1)/d>0
, the second statement shows that the giant still exists with stretched exponential error probability even when all vertices with mark above a constant are deleted from the graph. When
\zeta_{\star}=(d-1)/d
, this decay is even stronger than (
5.4
).

The requirement (
5.3
) corresponds to (
Req
) in Section
3
. It demands
some
initial density bound
\rho
on the size of the giant, and for the (hh) and (hl)-regimes it also demands that the highest-mark vertices are in the giant. Via edges incident to these high-mark vertices we can connect large components in subboxes in renormalization schemes below. We guarantee the presence of these long edges via the reasoning in
Section

2.2
. The requirement on high-mark vertices is not needed in the (ll) regime in (
5.1
), since we bound all marks from below by one. Thus, in the (ll)-regime, (
5.3
) is already satisfied for a small
\rho
by
Proposition

4.1
.
At the end of this section we verify (
5.3
) also for the (hl) and (hh)-regimes for a small
\rho
using Proposition
4.1
, obtaining
Corollary

5.7
.

We will apply
Proposition

5.1
again in Section
6.3
with density
\rho
close to
\theta
, as by then we have proved the weak LLN for
|{\mathcal{C}}_{n}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}|
for all parameters satisfying
\zeta_{\mathrm{long}}>0
. This will result in the upper bound on the right-hand side of (
2.21
) in
Theorem

2.7
.
The exponent
\zeta_{\star}
of the stretched exponential decay in
Proposition

5.1
will also appear in our upper bounds on
|{\mathcal{C}}_{n}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}|
and the cluster-size decay for
\zeta_{\mathrm{type}}\in\{\zeta_{\mathrm{hl}},\zeta_{\mathrm{ll}}\}
in Theorems
2.9
and
2.10
. Instead of
Proposition

5.1
,
Proposition

4.1
could instead be used as input to obtain (weaker) upper bounds on
|{\mathcal{C}}_{n}^{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}|
and the cluster-size decay for
\zeta_{\mathrm{type}}\in\{\zeta_{\mathrm{hl}},\zeta_{\mathrm{ll}}\}
. That strategy would still give a weak LLN and also Theorem
2.7
, but would lead to the appearance of an error
\delta
in exponents in Theorems
2.9
and
2.10
, which would then never match the lower bounds in Theorems
2.9
and
2.10
. This explains the benefit of
Proposition

5.1
.

Remark 5.2

(High-level comparison of proofs in Sections
4
and
5
)
.

Section
5
sets up a single-layer renormalization with carefully chosen number and size of boxes that lead exactly to (
5.4
) and (
5.5
), respectively. It needs Section
4
as an input, because in each of these boxes a linear-sized component has to be present whp on truncated mark vertices.

It is natural to ask why one could not use ‘more carefully chosen’ box sizes already in Section
4
and obtain better bounds already there? The rough answer is the following: here in Section
5
we shall shortly perform a single-layer renormalization. Since it is a single layer, we
can
afford to lose a small linear portion of the giant. Thus, we can renormalize to a
supercritical
Erdős-Rényi random graph (ERRG), and use large deviation results for those. Then, we have
\Theta(n^{\zeta})
many boxes, and the probability that a supercritical ERRG has a too small giant decays exponentially in the the number of boxes.

On the contrary, Section
4
uses an iterative scheme in order to improve the initial sublinear bound on the largest component to a linear bound. That is not possible in a single-layer renormalization. In an iterative scheme, one
cannot
allow to lose a small constant fraction of the largest component in each step. That would (again) result in a sublinear bound on the largest component. Therefore, in Section
4
we renormalized to a
connected
ERRG. For that we need slightly larger boxes, and thus we have
o(n^{\zeta})
boxes when reaching total size
n
. Moreover, the probability of the rare event that such an ERRG is not connected, decays exponentially in the expected degree, which is smaller than the total number of boxes. These reasons explain the
-\delta
term in the exponent in Section
4
.

We proceed to the proofs. We start with a claim that allows us to truncate the vertex marks from above; then we separately present the proof of the two statements in
Proposition

5.1
.

Claim 5.3
.

Consider a supercritical KSRG under the setting of Proposition
5.1
. Assume that
\zeta_{\mathrm{type}}>0
and (
5.3
) holds for some
\rho>0
,
\mathrm{type}\in\{\mathrm{hl},\mathrm{hh}\}
, and
\delta\in(0,1)
. Then, as
n
tends to infinity,

(5.6)

\mathbb{P}\Big(\exists\mbox{comp. ${\mathcal{C}}$ in ${\mathcal{G}}_{n}\Big[1,n^{\tfrac{1}{\tau-1}}\Big)$}:{\mathcal{C}}\supseteq{\mathcal{V}}\Big[n^{\tfrac{1-\delta}{\tau-1}},n^{\tfrac{1}{\tau-1}}\Big),|{\mathcal{C}}|>\rho n\Big)\longrightarrow 1.

Proof.

We abbreviate
\underline{w}=n^{(1-\delta)/(\tau-1)}
,
\overline{w}=n^{1/(\tau-1)}
throughout the proof.
We start from (
5.3
)
and distinguish whether
{\mathcal{G}}_{n}
contains vertices of mark at least
\overline{w}
: if there are no vertices of mark at least
\overline{w}
, then
{\mathcal{G}}_{n}[1,\overline{w})={\mathcal{G}}_{n}
. Slightly abusing standard notation, we write here
{\mathcal{C}}\subset{\mathcal{V}}_{n}[1,\underline{w})\cup{\mathcal{V}}_{n}[\underline{w},\infty)
for a set of vertices that is a
component
when restricted to marks below
\underline{w}
. Then we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}

\displaystyle\big(\exists\mbox{ comp. ${\mathcal{C}}$ in ${\mathcal{G}}_{n}$}:{\mathcal{C}}\supseteq{\mathcal{V}}[\underline{w},\infty),|{\mathcal{C}}|>\rho n\big)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\big(\{\exists\mbox{ comp. ${\mathcal{C}}$ in ${\mathcal{G}}_{n}[1,\overline{w})$}:{\mathcal{C}}\supseteq{\mathcal{V}}[\underline{w},\overline{w}),|{\mathcal{C}}|>\rho n\}\cap\{{\mathcal{V}}[\overline{w},\infty)=\emptyset\}\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 15.0pt+\mathbb{P}\big(\{\exists\mbox{ comp. ${\mathcal{C}}$ in ${\mathcal{G}}_{n}$}:{\mathcal{C}}\supseteq{\mathcal{V}}[\underline{w},\overline{w}),|{\mathcal{C}}|>\rho n\}\cap\{{\mathcal{V}}[\overline{w},\infty)\neq\emptyset\}\big).

Since the vertices are formed by a PPP,
{\mathcal{V}}[\overline{w},\infty)
is independent of
{\mathcal{V}}[1,\overline{w})
and thus also of the graph
{\mathcal{G}}_{n}[1,\overline{w})
. So, in the first term on the right-hand side we can take the product of the probabilities of the two events. Using the intensity of
{\mathcal{V}}
in (
4.5
),
\mathbb{P}({\mathcal{V}}[\overline{w},\infty)=\emptyset)=1/{\mathrm{e}}
. Hence, conditioning on
\{{\mathcal{V}}[\overline{w},\infty)\neq\emptyset\}
in the second term on the right-hand side yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}

\displaystyle\big(\exists\mbox{ comp. ${\mathcal{C}}$ in ${\mathcal{G}}_{n}$}:{\mathcal{C}}\supseteq{\mathcal{V}}[\underline{w},\overline{w}),|{\mathcal{C}}|>\rho n\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{{\mathrm{e}}}\mathbb{P}\big(\exists\mbox{ comp. ${\mathcal{C}}$ in ${\mathcal{G}}_{n}[1,\overline{w})$}:{\mathcal{C}}\supseteq{\mathcal{V}}[\underline{w},\overline{w}),|{\mathcal{C}}|>\rho n\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 15.0pt+\Big(1-\frac{1}{{\mathrm{e}}}\Big)\mathbb{P}\big(\exists\mbox{ comp. ${\mathcal{C}}$ in ${\mathcal{G}}_{n}$}:{\mathcal{C}}\supseteq{\mathcal{V}}[\underline{w},\overline{w}),|{\mathcal{C}}|>\rho n\mid{\mathcal{V}}[\overline{w},\infty)\neq\emptyset\big).

Since the left-hand side converges to
1
as
n
tends to infinity by the assumed condition (
5.3
), the two probabilities on the right-hand side must converge to
1
as well. Since the event in the first row on the right-hand side coincides with (
5.6
), the claim follows.
∎

5.1.
When relying on long edges

Before the proof of
Proposition

5.1
(long), we state a result about the giant in Erdős-Rényi random graph
G(n,p)
. The result will ‘replace’
Claim

4.7
in the renormalization scheme of this section compared to the scheme in Section
4
. The claim follows from a large-deviation principle for the size of the giant component obtained by O’Connell
[
68
]
, see also
[
9
]
. For our result, we do not need the exact rate function of this LDP.

Claim 5.4

(Large deviations in
G(n,\lambda/n)
)
.

Consider the largest connected component
C_{n}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}(p_{n})
of the Erdős-Rényi random graph
G(n,p_{n})
. Fix
\varepsilon>0
. There exist constants
A>0
,
\lambda=\lambda(\varepsilon)>1
such that for all
n\geq 1

\mathbb{P}\big(|C_{n}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}(\lambda/n)|<(1-\varepsilon)n\big)\leq\exp\big(-\tfrac{1}{A}n\big).

Proof.

Denote by
\varrho_{\lambda}
the survival probability of a Bienaymé-Galton-Watson branching process with
\mathrm{Poi}(\lambda)
offspring. By
[
68
, Theorem 3.1]
, for every
\lambda>1
and
\tilde{\varepsilon}>0
, there exists a constant
A>0
such that

\mathbb{P}\big(|C_{n}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}(\lambda/n)|<(1-\tilde{\varepsilon})\varrho_{\lambda}n\big)\leq\exp\big(-\tfrac{1}{A}n\big).

It is well known that the survival probability
\varrho_{\lambda}
tends to one as
\lambda\to\infty
, as
\varrho_{\lambda}
is the largest solution in
(0,1)
to the equation
\varrho_{\lambda}=1-\exp(-\lambda\varrho_{\lambda})
(see e.g.
[
70
, Example 6.1.10]
).
Hence, given
\varepsilon>0
, we may choose
\lambda
large enough, and
\tilde{\varepsilon}>0
small enough, so that
(1-\tilde{\varepsilon})\varrho_{\lambda}\geq(1-\varepsilon)
. The statement follows.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

5.1
(long).

We give the proof for KSRGs on a Poisson point process. The result for long-range percolation on
{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}
follows analogously to the case
\zeta_{\mathrm{ll}}>0
.

Fix
\zeta_{\mathrm{type}}\in\{\zeta_{\mathrm{hl}},\zeta_{\mathrm{hh}},\zeta_{\mathrm{ll}}\}
such that
\zeta_{\mathrm{type}}>0
. Let
\gamma_{\mathrm{type}}\in\{\gamma_{\mathrm{hl}},\gamma_{\mathrm{hh}},\gamma_{\mathrm{ll}}\}
be the corresponding value that solves
\zeta_{\mathrm{type}}=1-\gamma_{\mathrm{type}}(\tau-1)
by the definitions of
\gamma_{\mathrm{hl}}
,
\gamma_{\mathrm{hh}}
, and
\gamma_{\mathrm{ll}}
in (
2.11
), (
2.13
), and (
5.2
), respectively.
For readability, we omit the subscript
\mathrm{type}
throughout the proof.

Definitions of constants and boxing scheme.

Below we will suitably define a constant

M>0
depending on the regime (i.e., high-low, high-high, or low-low).
For simplicity, we assume that
(n^{\zeta}/M)^{1/d}\in{\mathbb{N}}
, so that we can partition
\Lambda_{n}
into

(5.7)

m_{n}:=n^{\zeta}/M

boxes
{\mathcal{Q}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{Q}}_{m_{n}}
. Using that
(1-{\zeta})/(\tau-1)=\gamma
, each box has volume

(5.8)

k_{n}:=n/m_{n}=Mn^{1-\zeta}=\big(M^{1/(\tau-1)}n^{\gamma}\big)^{\tau-1}.

Given any
\varepsilon>0
in
Proposition

5.1
(long), define the small
\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}>0
as the solutions of the following equations:

(5.9)

1-\varepsilon=(1-\varepsilon_{1})^{2},\qquad\mathbb{P}\big({\mathcal{V}}[\varepsilon_{2}k_{n}^{1/(\tau-1)},\infty)=\emptyset\big)=\varepsilon_{1}/4.

In a box of volume
k_{n}
, the expected number of vertices of mark at least
\varepsilon_{2}k_{n}^{1/(\tau-1)}
equals
\varepsilon_{2}^{-(\tau-1)}
by (
2.4
), so
\varepsilon_{2}
is a constant.

Good boxes and auxiliary graph.
We write
{\mathcal{V}}_{k_{n},j}:={\mathcal{V}}\cap({\mathcal{Q}}_{j}\times[1,\infty))
for the vertices in
{\mathcal{Q}}_{j}
,
{\mathcal{G}}_{k_{n},j}
for the subgraph of
{\mathcal{G}}_{n}
induced by the vertices in
{\mathcal{Q}}_{j}
, and similar to (
4.31
), we set

(5.10)

\text{pre-}{\mathcal{G}}_{n}:=\cup_{j\leq m_{n}}{\mathcal{G}}_{k_{n},j}.

We call a box
{\mathcal{Q}}_{j},j\in[m_{n}]

good
if the following event holds depending on the regime
\mathrm{type}\in\{\mathrm{hl},\mathrm{hh},\mathrm{ll}\}
.
When
\mathrm{type}\in\{\mathrm{hl},\mathrm{hh}\}
,

(5.11)

{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{box}}(j)\!:=\!\left\{\,\begin{aligned} \exists\mbox{comp. ${\mathcal{C}}_{j}$ in ${\mathcal{G}}_{k_{n},j}\big[1,k_{n}^{1/(\tau-1)}\big)$}:|{\mathcal{C}}_{j}|&\geq\rho k_{n},\\
{\mathcal{C}}_{j}&\supseteq{\mathcal{V}}_{k_{n},j}[\varepsilon_{2}k_{n}^{1/(\tau-1)},k_{n}^{1/(\tau-1)})\neq\emptyset\end{aligned}\right\}.

When
\mathrm{type}=\mathrm{ll}
,

{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{box}}(j):=\!\left\{\,\exists\mbox{comp. ${\mathcal{C}}_{j}$ in ${\mathcal{G}}_{k_{n},j}\big[1,k_{n}^{1/(\tau-1)}\big)$}:|{\mathcal{C}}_{j}|\geq\rho k_{n}\right\}.

We define the event that there are enough good boxes in
\Lambda_{n}
(similar to
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{sub}}
in
(
4.29
)):

(5.12)

{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{sub}}:=\bigg\{\sum_{j\in[m_{n}]}\mathds{1}_{\{{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{box}}(j)\}}\geq(1-\varepsilon_{1})m_{n}\bigg\},

The event
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{sub}}
is measurable with respect to pre-
{\mathcal{G}}_{n}
in (
5.10
).

Enough subboxes are good.
We estimate
\mathbb{P}\big(\neg{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{sub}}\big)
, with
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{sub}}
defined in (
5.12
).
For that we analyze the individual probabilities
\mathbb{P}\big({\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{box}}(j)\big)
. By the definition of
\varepsilon_{1}
in (
5.9
) and the hypothesis (
5.3
), for all
n
sufficiently large and any
j\in[m_{n}]

(5.13)

\mathbb{P}\big({\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{box}}(j)\big)\geq 1-\varepsilon_{1}/2.

Since the graphs
{\mathcal{G}}_{k_{n},j}
are iid across
j\in[m_{n}]
, by a standard Chernoff bound on binomial random variables, there exists a constant
c>0
such that

(5.14)

\mathbb{P}\big(\neg{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{sub}}\big)\leq\exp\big(-cm_{n}\big).

Introducing the auxiliary graph.

Similar to below (
4.31
) and (
4.43
), on any realization of pre-
{\mathcal{G}}_{n}
satisfying
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{sub}}
, we re-label boxes so that the first
\lceil(1-\varepsilon_{1})m_{n}\rceil
many boxes are good.
Then we define the auxiliary graph
{\mathcal{H}}_{n}=({\mathcal{V}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{n}},{\mathcal{E}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{n}})
on
\lceil(1-\varepsilon_{1})m_{n}\rceil
many vertices, so that vertex
j\in{\mathcal{V}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{n}}
corresponds to the component
{\mathcal{C}}_{j}
inside
{\mathcal{Q}}_{j}
in (
5.11
),
and two vertices
j,\ell
are connected in
{\mathcal{H}}_{n}
if the components
{\mathcal{C}}_{j}
and
{\mathcal{C}}_{\ell}
are connected by an edge in
{\mathcal{G}}_{n}
.
We write
{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{n}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}
for the largest component in
{\mathcal{H}}_{n}
.
By construction, the induced connected components
({\mathcal{C}}_{j})_{j\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{n}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}}
in (
5.11
) are all contained in the same component in
{\mathcal{G}}_{n}
. So if the event
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{sub}}\cap\{|{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{n}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}|\geq(1-\varepsilon_{1})|{\mathcal{V}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{n}}|\}
holds, then the graph
{\mathcal{G}}_{n}\big[1,k_{n}^{1/(\tau-1)}\big)
contains a connected component
{\mathcal{C}}
with

|{\mathcal{C}}|\geq\sum_{j\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{n}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}}|{\mathcal{C}}_{j}|\geq(1-\varepsilon_{1})^{2}m_{n}\cdot\rho k_{n}=(1-\varepsilon_{1})^{2}\rho n=(1-\varepsilon)\rho n.

Here we used that
|{\mathcal{C}}_{j}|\geq\rho k_{n}
by the event
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{box}}(j)
, that
k_{n}=n/m_{n}
by (
5.8
), and
(1-\varepsilon_{1})^{2}=1-\varepsilon
by (
5.9
).
Clearly, the largest component of
{\mathcal{G}}_{n}[1,k_{n}^{1/(\tau-1)})
is at least as large as
{\mathcal{C}}
, so

(5.15)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(|{\mathcal{C}}_{n}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}[1,k_{n}^{1/(\tau-1)})|\leq(1-\varepsilon)\rho n\big)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\big(\neg{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{sub}}\big)+\mathbb{P}\big({\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{sub}}\cap\{|{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{n}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}|\leq(1-\varepsilon)|{\mathcal{V}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{n}}|\}\big).

We study the two probabilities on the right-hand side separately.

Large deviation for the giant of ERRGs.
We turn to the second term on the right-hand side in (
5.15
). We rewrite this second term using the law of total probability: we first reveal pre-
{\mathcal{G}}_{n}
, then integrate over realizations satisfying
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{sub}}
:

(5.16)

\mathbb{P}\big({\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{sub}}\cap\{|{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{n}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}|\leq(1-\varepsilon)^{2}m_{n}\big)=\mathbb{E}\big[\mathds{1}_{\{{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{sub}}\}}\mathbb{P}\big(|{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{n}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}|\leq(1-\varepsilon_{1})|{\mathcal{V}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{n}}|\mid\text{pre-}{\mathcal{G}}_{n}\big)\big].

Let
\lambda=\lambda(\varepsilon_{1}),A>0
be the two constants from
Claim

5.4
so that for all
m\geq 1
,

(5.17)

\mathbb{P}\big(|C_{m}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}(\lambda/m)|<(1-\varepsilon_{1})m\big)\leq A\exp\big(-\tfrac{1}{A}m\big).

We will show below that conditionally on the realization pre-
\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{n}
satisfying
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{sub}}
,

(5.18)

{\mathcal{H}}_{n}\succcurlyeq G(|{\mathcal{V}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{n}}|,\lambda(\varepsilon_{1})/|{\mathcal{V}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{n}}|).

Suppose we have proven this stochastic domination. Then we can apply (
5.17
), and use that
|{\mathcal{V}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{n}}|=\lceil(1-\varepsilon_{1})m_{n}\rceil=:\widetilde{m}_{n}
:

\displaystyle\mathds{1}_{\{{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{sub}}\}}\mathbb{P}\big(|{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{n}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}|<(1-\varepsilon_{1})|{\mathcal{V}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{n}}|\mid\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{n}\big)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\big(|C_{\widetilde{m}_{n}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}(\lambda/\widetilde{m}_{n})|<(1-\varepsilon_{1})\widetilde{m}_{n}\big)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\big(-\tfrac{1}{A}|{\mathcal{V}}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{n}}|\big)\leq\exp(-\tfrac{1}{A(1-\varepsilon_{1})}m_{n})

This bound holds uniformly for all realizations of pre-
{\mathcal{G}}_{n}
with
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{sub}}
in (
5.16
). So, combined with (
5.14
), this bounds the two terms on the right-hand side in (
5.15
). Recalling that
m_{n}=n^{\zeta}/M
,

\mathbb{P}\big(|{\mathcal{C}}_{n}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}[1,k_{n}^{1/(\tau-1)})|<(1-\varepsilon)\rho n\big)\leq\exp\big(-\tfrac{c}{M}n^{\zeta}\big)+\exp\big(-\tfrac{1}{AM(1-\varepsilon_{1})}n^{\zeta}\big).

The mark bound
k_{n}^{1/(\tau-1)}
in the largest component on the left-hand side is equal to
M^{1/(\tau-1)}n^{\gamma}
by (
5.8
).
Thus, to finish the proof of (
5.4
