\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(\nexists{\mathcal{C}}:{\mathcal{C}}\not\nsupseteq{\mathcal{V}}_{n}[2A^{\prime}k^{\eta_{\mathrm{ll}}},\infty),|{\mathcal{C}}|\geq\rho n\big)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\bigg[|{\mathcal{V}}_{n}[2A^{\prime}k^{\eta_{\mathrm{ll}}},\infty)|\Big(1-p\Big(1\wedge\beta\frac{2A^{\prime}k^{\eta}}{d^{d/2}k}\Big)^{\alpha}\Big)^{\rho k}\bigg]+o(n^{-C})

\displaystyle\leq n\exp\big(-p\beta^{\alpha}d^{-\alpha d/2}\rho(2A^{\prime})^{\alpha}k^{\zeta}\big)+o(n^{-C}).

Here we used that
\eta=1/\alpha
implies that the minimum is again attained at the second term, and we then used that
\zeta=2-\alpha
. Substituting
k=(A_{1}\log n)^{1/(\zeta-\delta)}
yields that when
A_{1}
is large

\mathbb{P}\big(\nexists{\mathcal{C}}:{\mathcal{C}}\not\nsupseteq{\mathcal{V}}_{n}[2A^{\prime}k^{\eta_{\mathrm{ll}}},\infty),|{\mathcal{C}}|>\rho n\big)=o(n^{-C}),

showing (
5.36
) for some
A>0
.
The corresponding statements for the Palm version
\mathbb{P}^{\scriptscriptstyle x}
of
\mathbb{P}
for any
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, are proven in exactly the same way.
∎

Appendix C
Postponed proofs: upper tail

C.1.
Concentration of degrees and crossing edges

Proof of
Claim

7.3
.

We write
\underline{w}=\underline{w}_{n}
. To bound (
7.13
), we first distinguish whether there are more or fewer than
\lceil C/\psi\rceil
such vertices, and in the latter case we condition on their number:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Bigg(\sum_{v\in{\mathcal{V}}_{n}[\underline{w},\phi n)}\hskip-10.0pt\mathrm{deg}_{v}[1,\phi n)

\displaystyle\geq\psi n\Bigg)\leq\mathbb{P}\big(|{\mathcal{V}}_{n}[\underline{w},\phi n)|>\lceil C/\psi\rceil\big)

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}\bigg[\mathds{1}_{\{|{\mathcal{V}}_{n}[\underline{w},\phi n)|\leq\lceil C/\psi\rceil\}}\mathbb{P}\Bigg(\sum_{v\in{\mathcal{V}}_{n}[\underline{w},\phi n)}\hskip-10.0pt\mathrm{deg}_{v}[1,\phi n)\geq\psi n\,\Big|\,|{\mathcal{V}}_{n}[\underline{w},\phi n)|\Bigg)\bigg].

For the first probability, the intensity of
{\mathcal{V}}
in (
4.5
) yields that
|{\mathcal{V}}_{n}[\underline{w},\phi n)|\preccurlyeq\mathrm{Poi}(n\underline{w}^{-(\tau-1)})=\mathrm{Poi}(n^{-\psi})
. Hence,
\mathbb{P}(|{\mathcal{V}}_{n}[\underline{w},\phi n)|>\lceil C/\psi\rceil)=\Theta(n^{-\psi(\lceil C/\psi\rceil+1)})=o(n^{-C})
.
We turn to the term inside the expectation. For the sum
\sum_{v\in{\mathcal{V}}_{n}[\underline{w},\phi n)}\mathrm{deg}_{v}[1,\phi n)
to be at least
\psi n
, by the pigeon-hole principle, there must be a vertex among the at most
\lceil C/\psi\rceil
vertices in
{\mathcal{V}}_{n}[\underline{w},\phi n)
that has total degree at least
\psi n/\lceil(C/\psi)\rceil\geq 2n\psi^{2}/(3C)
(we may assume
C
large so that this inequality holds). Since at most
\lceil C/\psi\rceil
edges go to other vertices in
{\mathcal{V}}_{n}[\underline{w},\phi n)
, this vertex must have at least
2n\psi^{2}/(3C)-\lceil C/\psi\rceil\geq n\psi^{2}/(2C)
edges towards vertices with mark below
\underline{w}
(the bound holds for
n
large). We take a union bound over these high-mark vertices, and consider the worst-case location
y
of such a vertex to estimate the second term above:

(C.1)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Bigg(\sum_{v\in{\mathcal{V}}_{n}[\underline{w},\phi n)}

\displaystyle\mathrm{deg}_{v}[1,\phi n)\geq\psi n\Bigg)\leq o(n^{-C})

\displaystyle+\lceil\tfrac{C}{\psi}\rceil\sup_{(y,w)\in\Lambda_{n}\times[\underline{w},\phi n)}\mathbb{P}^{y}\Big(\mathrm{deg}_{(y,w)}[1,\underline{w})\geq\tfrac{\psi^{2}}{2C}n\mid(y,w)\in{\mathcal{V}}_{n}[\underline{w},\phi n)\Big).

Since
{\mathcal{V}}_{n}[1,\underline{w})
is independent of
{\mathcal{V}}_{n}[\underline{w},\infty)
, the PPP restricted to vertices of mark at most
\underline{w}
is independent of the location
y
of a high-mark vertex
v=(y,w)
. Since edges are present conditionally independently, conditionally on
v=(y,w)
, the neighbors of
v
in the graph form an inhomogeneous PPP on
\Lambda_{n}\times[1,\underline{w})\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{d+1}
with intensity
p\big((y,w),(x,z)\big)\big(\mathrm{d}x\times F_{W}(\mathrm{d}z)\big)
.
By integrating this intensity over
\Lambda_{n}\times[1,\underline{w})
(see
[
61
, Proposition 2.1]
for an example) the reader may verify that there exists a constant
c=c(d,\alpha,\tau,\sigma,\beta,p)>0
such that for
any
vertex
v=(y,w)
with location
y\in\Lambda_{n}
and
w\geq\underline{w}

\mathrm{deg}_{(y,w)}[1,\underline{w})\preccurlyeq\begin{cases}\mathrm{Poi}(cw),&\text{if }\sigma<\tau-1,\\
\mathrm{Poi}\big((cw\log(n/w)\big),&\text{if }\sigma=\tau-1,\\
\mathrm{Poi}\big(cw^{(\tau-1)/\sigma}n^{1-(\tau-1)/\sigma}\big),&\text{if }\sigma>\tau-1.\end{cases}

The intensity is increasing in
w
in all three cases, so the supremum in (
C.1
) is at
w=\phi n
. Substituting this value, the parameter of the Poisson distribution is order
O(n)
in all three cases with constant prefactor at most
c_{\phi}:=c\max(\phi,\phi^{(\tau-1)/\sigma},\phi\log(1/\phi))
. Clearly
c_{\phi}
tends to zero as
\phi\!\downarrow\!0
.
We
conclude that

\mathbb{P}\Bigg(\sum_{v\in{\mathcal{V}}_{n}[\underline{w},\phi n)}\hskip-10.0pt\mathrm{deg}_{v}[1,\phi n)\geq\psi n\Bigg)\leq o(n^{-C})+\lceil\tfrac{C}{\psi}\rceil\mathbb{P}\Big({\mathrm{Poi}}\big(nc_{\phi}\big)\geq n\frac{\psi^{2}}{2C}\Big).

By concentration for Poisson random variables (
Lemma

A.1
), the second term decays exponentially in
n
for any
\phi
sufficiently small, finishing the proof.
∎

Proof of
Claim

7.4
.

We first condition on the set of vertices with mark at least
\underline{w}
and apply a union bound over these vertices:

\mathbb{P}\big(\exists v\in{\mathcal{V}}_{n}[\underline{w},\infty):\mathrm{deg}_{v}[1,\underline{w})<\ell\big)\leq\mathbb{E}\bigg[\sum_{v\in{\mathcal{V}}_{n}[\underline{w},\infty)}\mathbb{P}\Big(\mathrm{deg}_{v}[1,\underline{w})<\ell\mid{\mathcal{V}}_{n}[\underline{w},\infty)\Big)\bigg].

We analyze a single summand: since the PPP
{\mathcal{V}}_{n}[1,\underline{w})
is independent of
{\mathcal{V}}_{n}[\underline{w},\infty)
, and edges are present conditionally independently, it follows that

(C.2)

\mathbb{P}\big(\mathrm{deg}_{v}[1,\underline{w})<\ell\mid{\mathcal{V}}_{n}[\underline{w},\infty)\big)\leq\sup_{(y,w)\in\Lambda_{n}\times[\underline{w},\infty)}\mathbb{P}\big(\mathrm{deg}_{v}[1,\underline{w})<\ell\mid v=(y,w)\in{\mathcal{V}}\big).

The neighbors of the vertex
v
form an independent inhomogeneous PPP on
\Lambda_{n}\times[1,\underline{w})
with intensity
p\big((y,w),(x,z)\big)\big(\mathrm{d}x\times F_{W}(\mathrm{d}z)\big)
.
By integrating the intensity over
\Lambda_{n}\times[1,\underline{w})
(see
[
61
, Proposition 2.1]
for an example) there exists a constant
c=c(d,\alpha,\tau,\sigma,\beta,p)>0
such that
\mathrm{deg}_{v}[1,\underline{w})\succcurlyeq\mathrm{Poi}(c(w\wedge n))
for
any

y\in\Lambda_{n}
. This is also true when
y
is close to the boundary of
\Lambda_{n}
, since at least a
d
-dependent constant fraction of the ball of volume
w\wedge n
centered at any
y
falls inside
\Lambda_{n}
. Since
w\geq\underline{w}
, the supremum in (
C.2
) is attained at
w=\underline{w}
, so we obtain that

\mathbb{P}\big(\exists v\in{\mathcal{V}}_{n}[\underline{w},\infty):\mathrm{deg}_{v}[1,\underline{w})<\ell\big)\leq\mathbb{E}[|{\mathcal{V}}_{n}[\underline{w},\infty)|]\mathbb{P}\big(\mathrm{Poi}(c\underline{w})<\ell\big)\leq n\mathbb{P}\big(\mathrm{Poi}(c\underline{w})<\ell\big).

By concentration inequalities for Poisson random variables (see
Lemma

A.1
), the probability on the right-hand side is of order
o(n^{-C-1})
when
\underline{w}\geq C_{1}\log n
, where
C_{1}=C_{1}(C,c)
is a sufficiently large constant.
∎

Proof of
Claim

7.5
.

We distinguish three types of vertices inside
\Lambda_{n}
that may be connected by an edge to a vertex outside
\Lambda_{n}
: (1) those close to the boundary of
\Lambda_{n}
, (2) those with mark at least a very large constant not close to the boundary, and (3) the rest. By the pigeon-hole principle, in order for the event inside of the probability of the statement (
7.14
) to hold, at least one of these three disjoint sets must have size at least
(\psi/3)n
. We write
\tilde{n}=(1-\psi/4)n
and
A_{0}:=\Lambda_{n}\setminus\Lambda_{\tilde{n}}
for an annulus inside
\Lambda_{n}
. Formally, the three cases become

(C.3)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(|\{v\in{\mathcal{V}}_{n}[1,\underline{w}):v

\displaystyle\sim\Lambda_{n}^{\mathsf{c}}\times[1,\underline{w})\}|\geq\psi n\big)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\big(|{\mathcal{V}}_{A_{0}}|\geq(\psi/3)n\big)+\mathbb{P}\big(|{\mathcal{V}}_{\tilde{n}}[(4/\psi)^{1/(\tau-1)},\infty)|\geq(\psi/3)n\big)

\displaystyle+\mathbb{P}\big(|\{v\in{\mathcal{V}}_{\tilde{n}}[1,(4/\psi)^{1/(\tau-1)}):v\sim\Lambda_{n}^{\mathsf{c}}\times[1,\underline{w})\}|\geq(\psi/3)n\big).

The size of the annulus in the first term and the mark-truncation value
(4/\psi)^{1/(\tau-1)}
in the second term are both chosen so that
\mathbb{E}[|{\mathcal{V}}_{A_{0}}|]=\mathbb{E}[|{\mathcal{V}}_{\tilde{n}}[(4/\psi)^{1/(\tau-1)},\infty)|]=(\psi/4)n
.
By concentration inequalities for Poisson variables (
Lemma

A.1
), the first two terms on the right-hand side decay exponentially in
n
and are thus
o(n^{-C})
. In the remainder, we focus on the third term.

When
\alpha=\infty
, the third term equals zero for
n
sufficiently large: by the connection probability
\mathrm{p}
in (
4.6
), a vertex with mark at most
(4/\psi)^{1/(\tau-1)}
cannot be connected by an edge to a vertex of mark at most
\underline{w}=o(n)
at distance
\Omega(n^{1/d})
.

We now study the third term in (
C.3
) when
\alpha<\infty
. We use a discretization argument: we decompose
\Lambda_{n}^{\mathsf{c}}\times[1,\underline{w})
into space-mark annuli and count edges according to where their endpoint falls. Set for
i\geq 1
and
j\leq\lceil\log_{2}(\underline{w})\rceil
,

(C.4)

\displaystyle A_{i,j}

\displaystyle:=(\Lambda_{2^{i}n}\setminus\Lambda_{2^{i-1}n})\times[2^{j},2^{j+1}),

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[{\mathcal{V}}\cap A_{i,j}]

\displaystyle=2^{i-1}n\cdot(2^{-j(\tau-1)}-2^{-(j+1)(\tau-1)})\leq 2^{i-j(\tau-1)-1}n.

To avoid too many crossing edges, a good event is when none of the sets
A_{i,j}
contains more than
2(\log n)^{2}
times the expectation many vertices. Truncating also at
1
, we define this event as

(C.5)

{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{out}}:=\bigcap_{i\geq 1}\bigcap_{j\leq\lceil\log_{2}(\underline{w})\rceil}{\mathcal{A}}_{i,j},\quad\mbox{with }\quad{\mathcal{A}}_{i,j}:=\Big\{|{\mathcal{V}}\cap A_{i,j}|\leq(\log n)^{2}\max(2^{i-j(\tau-1)}n,1\big)\Big\}.

Let us introduce the event that the number of vertices inside
\Lambda_{\tilde{n}}=\Lambda_{(1-\psi/4)n}
does not exceed
n
:

(C.6)

{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{in}}:=\{|{\mathcal{V}}_{\tilde{n}}[1,(4/\psi)^{1/(\tau-1)})|\leq n\}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad{\mathcal{A}}={\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{in}}\cap{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{out}}.

We first use concentration inequalities for Poisson random variables to show that
\mathbb{P}\big(\neg{\mathcal{A}})
decays superpolynomially.
To make another case distinction below, we let

(C.7)

\displaystyle j_{\ast}(i)

\displaystyle:=\max\big\{j\in[\lceil\log_{2}\underline{w}\rceil]:2^{i-j(\tau-1)-1}n\geq(\log n)^{2}\big\},

\displaystyle C_{1}

\displaystyle:=\inf_{C^{\prime}\geq 2\psi}\big\{\forall i\geq C_{1}\log_{2}n:j_{\ast}(i)=\lceil\log_{2}\underline{w}\rceil\big\}.

The number of vertices in
A_{i,j}
is Poisson distributed with expectation at most
2^{i-j(\tau-1)-1}n
, see (
C.4
).
Therefore, by a union bound over all space-mark annuli,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(\neg{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{out}}\big)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i\geq 1}\sum_{j=1}^{j_{\ast}(i)}\mathbb{P}\big(\mathrm{Poi}(2^{i-j(\tau-1)-1}n)\geq(\log n)^{2}2^{i-j(\tau-1)}n\big)

\displaystyle+\sum_{i=1}^{C_{1}\log_{2}n}\sum_{j=j_{\ast}(i)+1}^{\lceil\log_{2}\underline{w}\rceil}\mathbb{P}\Big(\mathrm{Poi}\big(2^{i-j(\tau-1)-1}n\big)\geq(\log n)^{2}\max\big(2^{i-j(\tau-1)}n,1\big)\Big).

We use that the function
f(\lambda):=\mathbb{P}({\mathrm{Poi}}(\lambda)>2(\log n)^{2}(\lambda\vee 1))
is increasing on the interval
[0,1]
, maximal at
\lambda=1
, and then decreasing on
[1,\infty)
. So, the summands on the second line can be all bounded from above by
\mathbb{P}\big(\mathrm{Poi}(1)\geq(\log n)^{2}\big)=O\big(1/(\log n)^{2}!\big)
. Since
\underline{w}=n^{(1+\psi)/(\tau-1)}
, there are
O((\log n)^{2})
summands, so the second row decays faster than any polynomial.
Turning to the first row, since the means here are all above
1
, for each fixed
i
, the largest summand is
j=j_{\ast}(i)\leq\lceil\log_{2}\underline{w}\rceil\leq\lceil\log_{2}n\rceil
. We then employ concentration for Poisson random variables (
Lemma

A.1
) and obtain that there exists
c>0
such that

\mathbb{P}\big(\neg{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{out}}\big)\leq o(n^{-C})+\sum_{i\geq 1}\Big(\lceil\log_{2}n\rceil\exp\big(-c2^{i-j_{\ast}(i)(\tau-1)}n\big)\Big).

If
i\geq\lceil C_{1}\log_{2}n\rceil
, then
j_{\ast}(i)=\lceil\log_{2}w\rceil
by definition in (
C.7
). For smaller
i
, again by definition of
j_{\ast}(i)
, the argument of the exponential is always at least
c(\log n)^{2}
. Therefore,

\mathbb{P}\big(\neg{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{out}}\big)\leq o(n^{-C})+C_{1}\lceil\log_{2}n\rceil^{2}\exp\big(-c(\log n)^{2}\big)+\lceil\log_{2}n\rceil\sum_{i\geq\lceil C_{1}\log_{2}n\rceil}\exp\big(-c2^{i-\lceil\log_{2}\underline{w}\rceil(\tau-1)}n\big).

The second term decays superpolynomially and is absorbed by the
o(n^{-C})
term. Since
\underline{w}\leq n^{(1+\psi)/(\tau-1)}
with
(1+\psi)/(\tau-1)<1
, we obtain for a constant
c^{\prime}>0
that

\mathbb{P}\big(\neg{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{out}}\big)\leq o(n^{-C})+\lceil\log_{2}n\rceil\sum_{i\geq\lceil C_{1}\log_{2}n\rceil}\exp\big(-c^{\prime}2^{i}n^{-\psi}\big).

Since
C_{1}>2\psi
by definition in (
C.7
), it follows that
2^{i}\geq n^{2\psi}
for all
i
. Therefore, the sum decays faster than any polynomial. We recall that
{\mathcal{A}}={\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{in}}\cap{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{out}}
by (
C.6
). By
Lemma

A.1
, also
\mathbb{P}\big(\neg{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{in}}\big)=\exp(-\Omega(n))
.
This proves that

(C.8)

\mathbb{P}\big(\neg{\mathcal{A}}\big)=o(n^{-C}).

We return to the third term in (
C.3
) and show that it decays exponentially in
n
when we condition on any realization of the vertex set
{\mathcal{V}}
that satisfies
{\mathcal{A}}
. Our first goal is to show that the probability that any fixed vertex
v\in{\mathcal{V}}_{\tilde{n}}[1,(4/\psi)^{1/(\tau-1)})
is connected by an edge to a vertex outside
\Lambda_{n}
tends to zero, conditionally on
{\mathcal{V}}
that satisfies
{\mathcal{A}}
.
Using the upper bounds on
|{\mathcal{V}}\cap A_{i,j}|
in (
C.5
) and the connection probability
\mathrm{p}
from (
4.6
), Markov’s inequality yields for any
v\in\Lambda_{\tilde{n}}\times[1,(4/\psi)^{1/(\tau-1)})
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(\exists u

\displaystyle\in\Lambda_{n}^{\mathsf{c}}\times[1,\underline{w}):v\sim u\mid{\mathcal{V}},{\mathcal{A}}\big)

\displaystyle\leq p\beta^{\alpha}(\log n)^{2}\sum_{i\geq 1}\sum_{j\in[\lceil\log_{2}\underline{w}\rceil]}\big(2^{i-j(\tau-1)}n\vee 1\big)\Big(\frac{\kappa_{\sigma}((C/\psi)^{1/(\tau-1)},2^{j+1})}{\|\Lambda_{\tilde{n}}-A_{i,j}\|^{d}}\Big)^{\alpha}.

By the definition of
\kappa_{\sigma}
in (
2.6
) the numerator is at most
c_{1}2^{j\alpha}
for some constant
c_{1}=c_{1}(C,\psi,\sigma,\alpha)
.
There exists a constant
c_{2}>0
such that
\|\Lambda_{\tilde{n}}-A_{i,j}\|\geq(c_{2}2^{i}n)^{1/d}
for all
i\geq 1
, and hence there exists another constant
c>0
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(\exists u\in\Lambda_{n}^{\mathsf{c}}\times[1,\underline{w}):v\sim u\mid{\mathcal{V}},{\mathcal{A}}\big)\leq c(\log n)^{2}n^{-\alpha}\sum_{i\geq 1}\sum_{j\in[\lceil\log_{2}\underline{w}\rceil]}\big(2^{i-j(\tau-1)}n\vee 1\big)2^{(j-i)\alpha}.

Studying the maximum inside the summation,
2^{i-j(\tau-1)}n<1
if
j\in[(i+\log_{2}n)/(\tau-1),\lceil\log_{2}\underline{w}\rceil]
. Since
\log_{2}\underline{w}\leq\log_{2}n
, this interval is non-empty only when
i<C_{2}\log_{2}n
for some constant
C_{2}>0
.
Using now that
x\vee 1\leq x+\mathds{1}_{\{x\leq 1\}}
with these criteria, we obtain the upper bound

(C.9)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(\exists u\in\Lambda_{n}^{\mathsf{c}}\times[1,\underline{w}):v\sim u\mid{\mathcal{V}},{\mathcal{A}}\big)

\displaystyle\leq c(\log n)^{2}n^{1-\alpha}\sum_{i\geq 1}\sum_{j\in[\lceil\log_{2}\underline{w}\rceil]}2^{-i(\alpha-1)+j(\alpha-(\tau-1))}

\displaystyle+c(\log n)^{2}n^{-\alpha}\sum_{i=1}^{\lceil C_{2}\log_{2}n\rceil}\sum_{j=\lceil(i+\log_{2}n)/(\tau-1)\rceil}^{\lceil\log_{2}\underline{w}\rceil}2^{(j-i)\alpha}.

We continue to bound the second line. Recall that
\underline{w}\leq n^{(1+\psi)/(\tau-1)}
where
(1+\psi)/(\tau-1)<1
. Therefore, there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that
2^{j}<n^{1-\varepsilon}
for all
j\leq\lceil\log_{2}\underline{w}\rceil
when
n
is sufficiently large. Furthermore, we bound
2^{-i\alpha}\leq 1
. Consequently, there exists
C_{3}>0
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(\exists u\in\Lambda_{n}^{\mathsf{c}}\times[1,\underline{w}):v\sim u\mid{\mathcal{V}},{\mathcal{A}}\big)

\displaystyle\leq c(\log n)^{2}n^{1-\alpha}\sum_{i\geq 1}2^{-i(\alpha-1)}\sum_{j\in[\lceil\log_{2}\underline{w}\rceil]}2^{j(\alpha-(\tau-1))}

\displaystyle+C_{3}(\log n)^{4}n^{-\varepsilon\alpha}.

We now bound the first line in (
C.9
) on the right-hand side. Since
\alpha>1
, the sum over
i
is finite. The sum over
j
is finite when
\alpha<\tau-1
, and at most logarithmic when
\alpha=\tau-1
. Since
\alpha>1
, in both of these cases the right-hand side on the first line is at most
O(n^{-\tilde{\varepsilon}})
for some
\tilde{\varepsilon}>0
. When
\alpha>\tau-1
, we use again that
2^{j}<n^{1-\varepsilon}
, so the maximal term is at most
n^{(1-\varepsilon)(\alpha-(\tau-1))}
. When multiplied with
n^{1-\alpha}
, this is at most
n^{-(\tau-2)-\varepsilon(\alpha-(\tau-1))}
. We conclude that in all cases there exist
\tilde{\varepsilon},C_{4}>0
such that

\mathbb{P}\big(\exists u\in\Lambda_{n}^{\mathsf{c}}[1,\underline{w}):v\sim u\mid{\mathcal{V}},{\mathcal{A}}\big)\leq C_{4}(\log n)^{3}n^{-\tilde{\varepsilon}}+C_{2}(\log n)^{4}n^{-\varepsilon\alpha}.

Assuming
n
is sufficiently large, the right-hand side is at most
\psi/6
.
By definition of
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{in}}
in (
C.6
) there are at most
n
vertices inside
{\mathcal{V}}_{\tilde{n}}[1,(4/\psi)^{1/(\tau-1)})
. Recall that edges are present conditionally independently on
{\mathcal{V}}
. Therefore, conditionally on any realization of
{\mathcal{V}}
that satisfies
{\mathcal{A}}
,

|\{v\in{\mathcal{V}}_{\tilde{n}}[1,(4/\psi)^{1/(\tau-1)}):v\sim\Lambda_{n}^{\mathsf{c}}\times[1,\underline{w})\}|\succcurlyeq\mathrm{Bin}(n,\psi/6).

Returning to the third term in (
C.3
), we obtain that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(|\{v\in{\mathcal{V}}_{\tilde{n}}[1,(4/\psi)^{1/(\tau-1)})

\displaystyle:v\sim\Lambda_{n}^{\mathsf{c}}\times[1,\underline{w})\}|\geq(\psi/3)n\big)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\big(\neg{\mathcal{A}})+\mathbb{E}\big[\mathds{1}_{\{{\mathcal{A}}\}}\mathbb{P}\big(\mathrm{Bin}(n,\psi/6)\geq(\psi/3)n\mid{\mathcal{V}},{\mathcal{A}}\big)\big].

The first term is of order
o(n^{-C})
by (
C.8
). The second term decays exponentially in
n
by a Chernoff bound. Thus all terms in (
C.3
) are of order
o(n^{-C})
, finishing the proof.
∎

C.2.
Truncation of the generating function

Proof of
Claim

7.7
.

We start with the first bound. Using elementary rearrangements of (
7.27
), we leave it to the reader to verify that it is sufficient that there exists
\psi^{\prime}>0
and
\ell_{0}
such that

\rho-\theta+\psi^{\prime}<\sum_{\ell=1}^{\ell_{0}}\big(1-(1-p)^{h_{\mathrm{lo}}\ell}\big)\theta_{\ell}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{\ell_{0}}\theta_{\ell}-\sum_{\ell=1}^{\ell_{0}}(1-p)^{h_{\mathrm{lo}}\ell}\theta_{\ell}.

Since
\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\theta_{\ell}=1-\theta
, we may choose
\ell_{0}
is sufficiently large (depending on
\psi^{\prime}
) so that
\sum_{\ell=1}^{\ell_{0}}\theta_{\ell}=\mathbb{P}^{\scriptscriptstyle 0}\big(|{\mathcal{C}}(0)|\leq\ell_{0}\big)>1-\theta-\psi^{\prime}
. Rearranging both sides, it is sufficient to show that we can choose
\psi^{\prime}
satisfying

(C.10)

2\psi^{\prime}<1-\rho-\sum_{\ell=1}^{\ell_{0}}(1-p)^{h_{\mathrm{lo}}\ell}\theta_{\ell}.

If
p=1
, then the sum is
0
and the bound is satisfied for any
\psi^{\prime}\in(0,(1-\rho)/2)
.
Now we assume that
p<1
, so that
\mathrm{hubs}(\rho)
, defined in (
2.17
), satisfies the equation

(C.11)

\mathbb{E}^{\scriptscriptstyle 0}\big[(1-p)^{\mathrm{hubs}(\rho)|{\mathcal{C}}(0)|}\big]=\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}(1-p)^{\mathrm{hubs}(\rho)\ell}\theta_{\ell}=1-\rho.

Now the definition of
h_{\mathrm{lo}}(\rho)
matters:
If
\mathrm{hubs}(\rho)
is an integer, then
h_{\mathrm{lo}}(\rho)=\lceil\mathrm{hubs}(\rho)\rceil+1=\mathrm{hubs}(\rho)+1
by definition of
h_{\mathrm{lo}}
in (
7.26
). If
\mathrm{hubs}(\rho)
is not an integer, then
h_{\mathrm{lo}}(\rho)=\lceil\mathrm{hubs}(\rho)\rceil
, and in both cases we have
h_{\mathrm{lo}}(\rho)>\mathrm{hubs}(\rho)
. So, using the infinite sum on the right-hand side of (
C.10
) in place of
1-\rho
gives that (
C.10
) holds if

2\psi^{\prime}<\sum_{\ell=1}^{\ell_{0}}\big((1-p)^{\mathrm{hubs}(\rho)\ell}-(1-p)^{h_{\mathrm{lo}}\ell}\big).

Since
\mathrm{hubs}(\rho)<h_{\mathrm{lo}}
, each summand is nonnegative, so the above bound is satisfied if
\psi^{\prime}
is chosen as, for instance,
\psi^{\prime}:=\big((1-p)^{\mathrm{hubs}(\rho)}-(1-p)^{h_{\mathrm{lo}}}\big)/2>0
.

We turn to the proof of (
7.28
) for which we first assume
p<1
.
Using elementary rearrangements, it suffices to find for an
\psi^{\prime}>0
sufficiently small, and an
\ell_{0}
large, so that for all
\ell^{\ast}\geq\ell_{0}
,

(C.12)

\sum_{\ell=1}^{\ell^{\ast}}\theta_{\ell}(1-p)^{(h_{\mathrm{up}}-1)\ell}>1-\rho+\psi^{\prime}.

Since we assumed
p<1
, the equality (
C.11
) is satisfied. Because
h_{\mathrm{up}}=\lceil\mathrm{hubs}(\rho)\rceil
, it holds that

h_{\mathrm{up}}-1<\mathrm{hubs}(\rho)
. Since the left-hand side in (
C.11
) is decreasing in
\mathrm{hubs}(\rho)
, it follows that

\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}(1-p)^{(h_{\mathrm{up}}-1)\ell}\theta_{\ell}>1-\rho.

Thus, there exists
\delta>0
, and
\ell_{0}>0
such that for all
\ell^{\ast}\geq\ell_{0}

\sum_{\ell=1}^{\ell^{\ast}}(1-p)^{(h_{\mathrm{up}}-1)\ell}\theta_{\ell}>1-\rho+\delta.

We combine this bound with (
C.12
), and choose
\psi^{\prime}=\delta
. This finishes the proof of (
7.28
) when
p<1
.
When
p=1
,
h_{\mathrm{up}}=1
by its own definition in (
7.26
) and the definition of
\mathrm{hubs}(\rho)=1
in (
2.17
). Hence,
(1-p)^{(h_{\mathrm{up}}-1)\ell}=1
for all
\ell\geq 1
. The proof follows as before since
\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\theta_{\ell}=1-\theta
, choose
\ell_{0}
is sufficiently large (depending on
\psi^{\prime}
) so that
\sum_{\ell=1}^{\ell_{0}}\theta_{\ell}=\mathbb{P}^{\scriptscriptstyle 0}\big(|{\mathcal{C}}(0)|\leq\ell_{0}\big)>1-\theta-\psi^{\prime}>1-\rho
.
∎
